========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hi, yeah, thank you for fitting me in. I'm... well, I came in because I just can’t shut my brain off anymore. It’s like the off switch is completely broken. I work as an IT systems manager, so I’m used to tracking a lot of moving parts and anticipating problems, but lately, my mind just takes off on its own and I absolutely cannot stop it. 

It usually starts with just one tiny, insignificant concern, you know? Like, say a client isn't responding to a routine email. Instead of just assuming they stepped away from their desk or went to lunch, within minutes I’m entirely convinced that they're furious, and that I’m going to lose the contract. And then, because I can't stop the train of thought, I start calculating how losing that contract means my department takes a hit, which means I'm going to fall behind on my divorce payments. And if I mess up those payments, my ex is going to drag me back to court, my finances will be completely ruined, and with that kind of hit to my life and reputation, I'll just never get back to a real leadership role again. 

I worked hard to get my degree and work my way up to management, and I just sit there watching my entire life collapse in my head over one unreturned email. But it feels so incredibly real in the moment, and I just sit there spiraling, going over every catastrophic detail. It just goes on and on like that, and I can't seem to make it stop.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, it’s definitely not just social situations. In fact, being alone with routine tasks is sometimes the absolute worst time for it, because there's absolutely no buffer, you know? I don't have to keep up a professional face, so the thoughts just run completely wild. I could be sitting completely by myself in my home office, just running a standard system update or checking server logs—something I've done a million times—and my mind keeps jumping from one catastrophic scenario to the next. 

It never just stays on the task in front of me. I’ll be looking at a screen, and suddenly I'm worrying about my current contract work, and then my brain just immediately leaps over to my mortgage payments and whether I'm going to default. And then... well, then it gets into this whole other layer where I start agonizing over my reputation. I'll sit there alone and start obsessing over whether people in the Bethesda tech circles think I’m damaged goods now because of the divorce or if they think I'm losing my edge. 

It’s just this constant, exhausting chain reaction. Like, just last week I was at home, by myself, waiting on what should have been a very standard, routine email from a recruiter. Nothing crazy. But it was delayed. And instead of just thinking, 'Oh, they're in a meeting,' I sat there in my kitchen and spiraled to the point where that one delayed email felt like absolute, undeniable proof that my whole career is closing down. I literally convinced myself my professional life was over, just sitting there alone with my laptop. So, yeah, I don't need to be at a networking event or a crowded meeting for this to happen. The worry just finds me wherever I am, whatever I'm doing, and it just bounces from my finances to my career to my reputation without giving me a single second to breathe.

Oh, absolutely. It's not just in my head, it's... it's like my whole body is involved in this, you know? The tension is just constant. I feel completely wound up almost all day long. My shoulders, for instance—they are just locked tight up around my ears from the minute I wake up. It’s like I’m physically bracing myself, just constantly ready for some terrible news to drop, even when there's absolutely no reason to expect any. 

And the restlessness, it’s just... it's exhausting. I can't just sit still anymore. If I'm on a conference call—even a totally routine one where I’m just listening in and nothing is actually happening—I’ll be sitting there just bouncing my leg up and down like a jackhammer under the desk. Or if I'm off camera, I'll literally just pace back and forth across my home office. I'll walk from the desk to the window, back to the desk, into the hallway, just pacing and pacing while I'm trying to listen to server updates or whatever. 

It’s like there’s this electric current just running through me all the time and I have to move to bleed some of it off, but it never really goes away. I just feel so physically keyed up, like a spring wound way too tight, and no matter how much I pace around the house or try to stretch out my neck and shoulders, that locked-in, braced feeling just stays right there. It's like my body is reacting to an emergency that isn't even happening, and I just can't get it to stand down.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I mean... yes, absolutely. It's exactly like that. It's just this constant, unending barrage of things to worry about, and I really, truly cannot control it. I try, I really do. I'll tell myself, 'Okay, just focus on this one task,' but it never works. It's like my brain has a mind of its own, if that makes sense. 

Like I was saying before, it's never just one thing. My mind keeps jumping from my current contract work, which is stressful enough on its own, right? But then it doesn't stop there. It immediately leaps over to my mortgage payments, and I start doing all this math in my head about what happens if a contract falls through and I can't make the payment, and then I'm picturing foreclosure. And then, as if that's not enough, it pivots entirely to my reputation. I'll just sit there obsessing over whether people in the Bethesda tech circles—which is a really tight-knit community, everybody talks, you know?—I'll worry that they think I’m damaged goods now because of the messy divorce, or that they think I'm losing my edge because I'm distracted. 

It just bounces from one disaster to the next. And there doesn't even need to be a real reason for it! Like that recruiter email I mentioned. It was just delayed. Probably the guy was just at lunch or stuck on a call. But to me, in that moment? That delayed email felt like absolute, undeniable proof that my whole career is closing down. I sat there and basically planned out my entire professional funeral just because an email was an hour late. And I know how irrational that sounds when I say it out loud to you, I really do. I'm a logical guy, I work in IT, I deal with facts and systems. But when the worry hits, all that logic just goes right out the window, and I can't stop the spiral no matter how hard I try to reason with myself.

Like, just last week I was at home, by myself, waiting on what should have been a very standard, routine email from a recruiter. Nothing crazy. But it was delayed. And instead of just thinking, 'Oh, they're in a meeting,' I sat there in my kitchen and spiraled to the point where that one delayed email felt like absolute, undeniable proof that my whole career is closing down. I literally convinced myself my professional life was over, just sitting there alone with my laptop. So, yeah, I don't need to be at a networking event or a crowded meeting for this to happen. The worry just finds me wherever I am, whatever I'm doing, and it just bounces from my finances to my career to my reputation without giving me a single second to breathe.\n\nDoctor: When these anxious thoughts come up, do you also notice any physical symptoms, like restlessness, trouble sleeping, muscle tension, or a racing heart?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. It's not just in my head, it's... it's like my whole body is involved in this, you know? The tension is just constant. I feel completely wound up almost all day long. My shoulders, for instance—they are just locked tight up around my ears from the minute I wake up. It’s like I’m physically bracing myself, just constantly ready for some terrible news to drop, even when there's absolutely no reason to expect any. \n\nAnd the restlessness, it’s just... it's exhausting. I can't just sit still anymore. If I'm on a conference call—even a totally routine one where I’m just listening in and nothing is actually happening—I’ll be sitting there just bouncing my leg up and down like a jackhammer under the desk. Or if I'm off camera, I'll literally just pace back and forth across my home office. I'll walk from the desk to the window, back to the desk, into the hallway, just pacing and pacing while I'm trying to listen to server updates or whatever. \n\nIt’s like there’s this electric current just running through me all the time and I have to move to bleed some of it off, but it never really goes away. I just feel so physically keyed up, like a spring wound way too tight, and no matter how much I pace around the house or try to stretch out my neck and shoulders, that locked-in, braced feeling just stays right there. It's like my body is reacting to an emergency that isn't even happening, and I just can't get it to stand down.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you worry excessively about a variety of things in your life, even when there's no clear reason to be concerned, and does it feel like this worry is hard to control or stop, even when you try?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... yes, absolutely. It's exactly like that. It's just this constant, unending barrage of things to worry about, and I really, truly cannot control it. I try, I really do. I'll tell myself, 'Okay, just focus on this one task,' but it never works. It's like my brain has a mind of its own, if that makes sense. \n\nLike I was saying before, it's never just one thing. My mind keeps jumping from my current contract work, which is stressful enough on its own, right? But then it doesn't stop there. It immediately leaps over to my mortgage payments, and I start doing all this math in my head about what happens if a contract falls through and I can't make the payment, and then I'm picturing foreclosure. And then, as if that's not enough, it pivots entirely to my reputation. I'll just sit there obsessing over whether people in the Bethesda tech circles—which is a really tight-knit community, everybody talks, you know?—I'll worry that they think I’m damaged goods now because of the messy divorce, or that they think I'm losing my edge because I'm distracted. \n\nIt just bounces from one disaster to the next. And there doesn't even need to be a real reason for it! Like that recruiter email I mentioned. It was just delayed. Probably the guy was just at lunch or stuck on a call. But to me, in that moment? That delayed email felt like absolute, undeniable proof that my whole career is closing down. I sat there and basically planned out my entire professional funeral just because an email was an hour late. And I know how irrational that sounds when I say it out loud to you, I really do. I'm a logical guy, I work in IT, I deal with facts and systems. But when the worry hits, all that logic just goes right out the window, and I can't stop the spiral no matter how hard I try to reason with myself.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, if I really trace it back... it’s been a little over a year now. Thirteen months, to be exact. Thirteen months of just... this. That was when I got let go from my old firm. I was fired, and honestly, that was the absolute catalyst for all of this. I had been there for a long time, you know? I had built up my department, I knew the systems inside and out, and then suddenly—just, bam, I'm out. The floor completely dropped out from under me. 

At first, I thought the constant worrying was just a normal reaction to losing my job. I mean, anyone would be stressed about that, right? You lose your primary income, you lose your daily routine, you immediately start doing the math on how many months you can survive before the savings run dry. I remember sitting at my kitchen table the morning after it happened, just staring at my laptop, trying to figure out how to even begin updating my resume after all those years, and that's when this... this engine of worry just turned on in my head. 

But the craziest part, and the part that really exhausts me, is that I thought it would stop once I got back on my feet. I really, truly believed that. I told myself, 'Okay, I just need to find a new gig, get some income flowing again, and my brain will finally settle down.' But I did find work. I transitioned over to doing contract work, managing some system migrations on a freelance basis, and it keeps the lights on. But the worry? It never let up. Not even a fraction of a bit. It didn't go away when I signed that first contract. 

If anything, it just shifted its focus. Instead of worrying about not having a job at all, now I'm obsessing over the contract ending early, or the client suddenly deciding they hate my work, or an invoice getting held up in accounting and causing me to miss a mortgage payment. It's like being fired thirteen months ago just permanently broke some kind of switch in my brain, and now it's stuck in the 'on' position no matter what my actual employment status is. Even with the contract work coming in, the dread is just exactly the same as it was the day I lost my job. I just can't seem to turn it off.

It’s like the off switch is completely broken. I work as an IT systems manager, so I’m used to tracking a lot of moving parts and anticipating problems, but lately, my mind just takes off on its own and I absolutely cannot stop it. \n\nIt usually starts with just one tiny, insignificant concern, you know? Like, say a client isn't responding to a routine email. Instead of just assuming they stepped away from their desk or went to lunch, within minutes I’m entirely convinced that they're furious, and that I’m going to lose the contract. And then, because I can't stop the train of thought, I start calculating how losing that contract means my department takes a hit, which means I'm going to fall behind on my divorce payments. And if I mess up those payments, my ex is going to drag me back to court, my finances will be completely ruined, and with that kind of hit to my life and reputation, I'll just never get back to a real leadership role again. \n\nI worked hard to get my degree and work my way up to management, and I just sit there watching my entire life collapse in my head over one unreturned email. But it feels so incredibly real in the moment, and I just sit there spiraling, going over every catastrophic detail. It just goes on and on like that, and I can't seem to make it stop.\n\nDoctor: Do these anxious thoughts happen mostly in social situations, or do they come up even when you're alone or dealing with routine tasks on your own?\n\nPatient: Oh, it’s definitely not just social situations. In fact, being alone with routine tasks is sometimes the absolute worst time for it, because there's absolutely no buffer, you know? I don't have to keep up a professional face, so the thoughts just run completely wild. I could be sitting completely by myself in my home office, just running a standard system update or checking server logs—something I've done a million times—and my mind keeps jumping from one catastrophic scenario to the next. \n\nIt never just stays on the task in front of me. I’ll be looking at a screen, and suddenly I'm worrying about my current contract work, and then my brain just immediately leaps over to my mortgage payments and whether I'm going to default. And then... well, then it gets into this whole other layer where I start agonizing over my reputation. I'll sit there alone and start obsessing over whether people in the Bethesda tech circles think I’m damaged goods now because of the divorce or if they think I'm losing my edge. \n\nIt’s just this constant, exhausting chain reaction. Like, just last week I was at home, by myself, waiting on what should have been a very standard, routine email from a recruiter. Nothing crazy. But it was delayed. And instead of just thinking, 'Oh, they're in a meeting,' I sat there in my kitchen and spiraled to the point where that one delayed email felt like absolute, undeniable proof that my whole career is closing down. I literally convinced myself my professional life was over, just sitting there alone with my laptop. So, yeah, I don't need to be at a networking event or a crowded meeting for this to happen. The worry just finds me wherever I am, whatever I'm doing, and it just bounces from my finances to my career to my reputation without giving me a single second to breathe.\n\nDoctor: When these anxious thoughts come up, do you also notice any physical symptoms, like restlessness, trouble sleeping, muscle tension, or a racing heart?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. It's not just in my head, it's... it's like my whole body is involved in this, you know? The tension is just constant. I feel completely wound up almost all day long. My shoulders, for instance—they are just locked tight up around my ears from the minute I wake up. It’s like I’m physically bracing myself, just constantly ready for some terrible news to drop, even when there's absolutely no reason to expect any. \n\nAnd the restlessness, it’s just... it's exhausting. I can't just sit still anymore. If I'm on a conference call—even a totally routine one where I’m just listening in and nothing is actually happening—I’ll be sitting there just bouncing my leg up and down like a jackhammer under the desk. Or if I'm off camera, I'll literally just pace back and forth across my home office. I'll walk from the desk to the window, back to the desk, into the hallway, just pacing and pacing while I'm trying to listen to server updates or whatever. \n\nIt’s like there’s this electric current just running through me all the time and I have to move to bleed some of it off, but it never really goes away. I just feel so physically keyed up, like a spring wound way too tight, and no matter how much I pace around the house or try to stretch out my neck and shoulders, that locked-in, braced feeling just stays right there. It's like my body is reacting to an emergency that isn't even happening, and I just can't get it to stand down.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you worry excessively about a variety of things in your life, even when there's no clear reason to be concerned, and does it feel like this worry is hard to control or stop, even when you try?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... yes, absolutely. My mind keeps jumping from my current contract work, which is stressful enough on its own, right? But then it doesn't stop there. And then, as if that's not enough, it pivots entirely to my reputation. I'll just sit there obsessing over whether people in the Bethesda tech circles—which is a really tight-knit community, everybody talks, you know?—I'll worry that they think I’m damaged goods now because of the messy divorce, or that they think I'm losing my edge because I'm distracted. \n\nIt just bounces from one disaster to the next. It immediately leaps over to my mortgage payments, and I start doing all this math in my head about what happens if a contract falls through and I can't make the payment, and then I'm picturing foreclosure. And then, as if that's not enough, it pivots entirely to my reputation. I'll just sit there obsessing over whether people in the Bethesda tech circles—which is a really tight-knit community, everybody talks, you know?—I'll worry that they think I’m damaged goods now because of the messy divorce, or that they think I'm losing my edge because I'm distracted. \n\nIt just bounces from one disaster to the next. And there doesn't even need to be a real reason for it! Like that recruiter email I mentioned. It was just delayed. Probably the guy was just at lunch or stuck on a call. But to me, in that moment? That delayed email felt like absolute, undeniable proof that my whole career is closing down. I sat there and basically planned out my entire professional funeral just because an email was an hour late. And I know how irrational that sounds when I say it out loud to you, I really do. I'm a logical guy, I work in IT, I deal with facts and systems. But when the worry hits, all that logic just goes right out the window, and I can't stop the spiral no matter how hard I try to reason with myself.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this constant and uncontrollable worry, and was there a specific event or change in your life that seemed to trigger it?\n\nPatient: Well, if I really trace it back... it’s been a little over a year now. Thirteen months, to be exact. Thirteen months of just... this. That was when I got let go from my old firm. I was fired, and honestly, that was the absolute catalyst for all of this. I had been there for a long time, you know? I had built up my department, I knew the systems inside and out, and then suddenly—just, bam, I'm out. The floor completely dropped out from under me. \n\nAt first, I thought the constant worrying was just a normal reaction to losing my job. I mean, anyone would be stressed about that, right? You lose your primary income, you lose your daily routine, you immediately start doing the math on how many months you can survive before the savings run dry. I remember sitting at my kitchen table the morning after it happened, just staring at my laptop, trying to figure out how to even begin updating my resume after all those years, and that's when this... this engine of worry just turned on in my head. \n\nBut the craziest part, and the part that really exhausts me, is that I thought it would stop once I got back on my feet. I really, truly believed that. I told myself, 'Okay, I just need to find a new gig, get some income flowing again, and my brain will finally settle down.' But I did find work. I transitioned over to doing contract work, managing some system migrations on a freelance basis, and it keeps the lights on. But the worry? It never let up. Not even a fraction of a bit. It didn't go away when I signed that first contract. \n\nIf anything, it just shifted its focus. Instead of worrying about not having a job at all, now I'm obsessing over the contract ending early, or the client suddenly deciding they hate my work, or an invoice getting held up in accounting and causing me to miss a mortgage payment. It's like being fired thirteen months ago just permanently broke some kind of switch in my brain, and now it's stuck in the 'on' position no matter what my actual employment status is. Even with the contract work coming in, the dread is just exactly the same as it was the day I lost my job. I just can't seem to turn it off.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you start feeling anxious or overwhelmed by these thoughts, do you find yourself actively avoiding certain situations, tasks, or even people because you think they might trigger more of these worries?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I do. All the time now. It's actually becoming a huge problem, both professionally and personally, and I just... I don't know how to break out of it. 

Like with the contract work I was just telling you about... I'll have a deliverable due, right? Something standard, like a system architecture review or a migration plan. I know how to do it, I've done a million of them in my career. But I'll sit down to send the final file, and my brain just starts screaming at me. 'What if you missed a critical vulnerability? What if the client hates the formatting? What if they think your work is sloppy and they tell everyone in the Bethesda tech loop?' So... I just don't send it. I'll tell myself I just need to review it one more time tomorrow. And then tomorrow comes, and I do the exact same thing. I’ve literally delayed client deliverables for days because I just get completely stuck, paralyzed really, anticipating every single little thing that could possibly go wrong. 

And networking? Forget about it. In this freelance game, you have to network, but I've missed so many crucial follow-ups. I'll get someone's contact info, and then I'll just stare at a blank email draft for an hour, convinced that whatever I write is going to make me sound desperate or incompetent, until I just close the window and avoid them entirely. 

And the worst part is that it’s not just work. It’s bleeding into everything else. I used to play in this adult rec soccer league. Nothing crazy, just a bunch of guys trying not to pull a hamstring on Thursday nights, you know? We’d play, maybe grab a beer after. I've played with some of these guys for years. But I’ve completely pulled back from that. I haven't gone in over a month, and I've basically ghosted my friends there. 

And honestly... it's because I’m just so embarrassed. I'm embarrassed by who I am right now. Because I'm so wound up and exhausted all the time, I've gotten incredibly tense and irritable. I'll be out on the field and someone will miss a pass or make a stupid joke, and I'll just feel this massive flash of anger and snap at them. Or we'll be sitting around after the game, and I can't even participate in the conversation because I'm sitting there bouncing my leg, secretly doing mortgage math in my head, totally checked out. I just feel like I'm horrible to be around right now, so it's easier to just avoid them. To avoid everything. But then, of course, I sit at home alone and worry about the fact that I'm alienating all my friends, which just makes the whole thing worse. It's just this endless loop.

And if I mess up those payments, my ex is going to drag me back to court, my finances will be completely ruined, and with that kind of hit to my life and reputation, I'll just never get back to a real leadership role again. \n\nI worked hard to get my degree and work my way up to management, and I just sit there watching my entire life collapse in my head over one unreturned email. But it feels so incredibly real in the moment, and I just sit there spiraling, going over every catastrophic detail. It just goes on and on like that, and I can't seem to make it stop.\n\nDoctor: Do these anxious thoughts happen mostly in social situations, or do they come up even when you're alone or dealing with routine tasks on your own?\n\nPatient: Oh, it’s definitely not just social situations. In fact, being alone with routine tasks is sometimes the absolute worst time for it, because there's absolutely no buffer, you know? I don't have to keep up a professional face, so the thoughts just run completely wild. I could be sitting completely by myself in my home office, just running a standard system update or checking server logs—something I've done a million times—and my mind keeps jumping from one catastrophic scenario to the next. \n\nIt never just stays on the task in front of me. I’ll be looking at a screen, and suddenly I'm worrying about my current contract work, and then my brain just immediately leaps over to my mortgage payments and whether I'm going to default. And then... well, then it gets into this whole other layer where I start agonizing over my reputation. I'll sit there alone and start obsessing over whether people in the Bethesda tech circles think I’m damaged goods now because of the divorce or if they think I'm losing my edge. \n\nIt’s just this constant, exhausting chain reaction. Like, just last week I was at home, by myself, waiting on what should have been a very standard, routine email from a recruiter. Nothing crazy. But it was delayed. And instead of just thinking, 'Oh, they're in a meeting,' I sat there in my kitchen and spiraled to the point where that one delayed email felt like absolute, undeniable proof that my whole career is closing down. I literally convinced myself my professional life was over, just sitting there alone with my laptop. So, yeah, I don't need to be at a networking event or a crowded meeting for this to happen. The worry just finds me wherever I am, whatever I'm doing, and it just bounces from my finances to my career to my reputation without giving me a single second to breathe.\n\nDoctor: When these anxious thoughts come up, do you also notice any physical symptoms, like restlessness, trouble sleeping, muscle tension, or a racing heart?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. It's not just in my head, it's... it's like my whole body is involved in this, you know? The tension is just constant. I feel completely wound up almost all day long. My shoulders, for instance—they are just locked tight up around my ears from the minute I wake up. It’s like I’m physically bracing myself, just constantly ready for some terrible news to drop, even when there's absolutely no reason to expect any. \n\nAnd the restlessness, it’s just... it's exhausting. I can't just sit still anymore. If I'm on a conference call—even a totally routine one where I’m just listening in and nothing is actually happening—I’ll be sitting there just bouncing my leg up and down like a jackhammer under the desk. Or if I'm off camera, I'll literally just pace back and forth across my home office. I'll walk from the desk to the window, back to the desk, into the hallway, just pacing and pacing while I'm trying to listen to server updates or whatever. \n\nIt’s like there’s this electric current just running through me all the time and I have to move to bleed some of it off, but it never really goes away. I just feel so physically keyed up, like a spring wound way too tight, and no matter how much I pace around the house or try to stretch out my neck and shoulders, that locked-in, braced feeling just stays right there. It's like my body is reacting to an emergency that isn't even happening, and I just can't get it to stand down.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you worry excessively about a variety of things in your life, even when there's no clear reason to be concerned, and does it feel like this worry is hard to control or stop, even when you try?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... yes, absolutely. It's exactly like that. It's just this constant, unending barrage of things to worry about, and I really, truly cannot control it. I try, I really do. I'll tell myself, 'Okay, just focus on this one task,' but it never works. It's like my brain has a mind of its own, if that makes sense. \n\nLike I was saying before, it's never just one thing. My mind keeps jumping from my current contract work, which is stressful enough on its own, right? But then it doesn't stop there. It immediately leaps over to my mortgage payments, and I start doing all this math in my head about what happens if a contract falls through and I can't make the payment, and then I'm picturing foreclosure. And then, as if that's not enough, it pivots entirely to my reputation. I'll just sit there obsessing over whether people in the Bethesda tech circles—which is a really tight-knit community, everybody talks, you know?—I'll worry that they think I’m damaged goods now because of the messy divorce, or that they think I'm losing my edge because I'm distracted. \n\nIt just bounces from one disaster to the next. And there doesn't even need to be a real reason for it! Like that recruiter email I mentioned. It was just delayed. Probably the guy was just at lunch or stuck on a call. But to me, in that moment? That delayed email felt like absolute, undeniable proof that my whole career is closing down. I sat there and basically planned out my entire professional funeral just because an email was an hour late. And I know how irrational that sounds when I say it out loud to you, I really do. I'm a logical guy, I work in IT, I deal with facts and systems. But when the worry hits, all that logic just goes right out the window, and I can't stop the spiral no matter how hard I try to reason with myself.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this constant and uncontrollable worry, and was there a specific event or change in your life that seemed to trigger it?\n\nPatient: Well, if I really trace it back... it’s been a little over a year now. Thirteen months, to be exact. Thirteen months of just... this. That was when I got let go from my old firm. I was fired, and honestly, that was the absolute catalyst for all of this. I had been there for a long time, you know? I had built up my department, I knew the systems inside and out, and then suddenly—just, bam, I'm out. The floor completely dropped out from under me. \n\nAt first, I thought the constant worrying was just a normal reaction to losing my job. I mean, anyone would be stressed about that, right? You lose your primary income, you lose your daily routine, you immediately start doing the math on how many months you can survive before the savings run dry. I remember sitting at my kitchen table the morning after it happened, just staring at my laptop, trying to figure out how to even begin updating my resume after all those years, and that's when this... this engine of worry just turned on in my head. \n\nBut the craziest part, and the part that really exhausts me, is that I thought it would stop once I got back on my feet. I really, truly believed that. I told myself, 'Okay, I just need to find a new gig, get some income flowing again, and my brain will finally settle down.' But I did find work. I transitioned over to doing contract work, managing some system migrations on a freelance basis, and it keeps the lights on. But the worry? It never let up. Not even a fraction of a bit. It didn't go away when I signed that first contract. \n\nIf anything, it just shifted its focus. Instead of worrying about not having a job at all, now I'm obsessing over the contract ending early, or the client suddenly deciding they hate my work, or an invoice getting held up in accounting and causing me to miss a mortgage payment. It's like being fired thirteen months ago just permanently broke some kind of switch in my brain, and now it's stuck in the 'on' position no matter what my actual employment status is. Even with the contract work coming in, the dread is just exactly the same as it was the day I lost my job. I just can't seem to turn it off.\n\nDoctor: When you start feeling anxious or overwhelmed by these thoughts, do you find yourself actively avoiding certain situations, tasks, or even people because you think they might trigger more of these worries?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. All the time now. It's actually becoming a huge problem, both professionally and personally, and I just... I don't know how to break out of it. \n\nLike with the contract work I was just telling you about... I'll have a deliverable due, right? Something standard, like a system architecture review or a migration plan. I know how to do it, I've done a million of them in my career. But I'll sit down to send the final file, and my brain just starts screaming at me. 'What if you missed a critical vulnerability? What if the client hates the formatting? What if they think your work is sloppy and they tell everyone in the Bethesda tech loop?' So... I just don't send it. I'll tell myself I just need to review it one more time tomorrow. And then tomorrow comes, and I do the exact same thing. I’ve literally delayed client deliverables for days because I just get completely stuck, paralyzed really, anticipating every single little thing that could possibly go wrong. \n\nAnd networking? Forget about it. In this freelance game, you have to network, but I've missed so many crucial follow-ups. I'll get someone's contact info, and then I'll just stare at a blank email draft for an hour, convinced that whatever I write is going to make me sound desperate or incompetent, until I just close the window and avoid them entirely. \n\nAnd the worst part is that it’s not just work. It’s bleeding into everything else. I used to play in this adult rec soccer league. Nothing crazy, just a bunch of guys trying not to pull a hamstring on Thursday nights, you know? We’d play, maybe grab a beer after. I've played with some of these guys for years. But I’ve completely pulled back from that. I haven't gone in over a month, and I've basically ghosted my friends there. \n\nAnd honestly... it's because I’m just so embarrassed. I'm embarrassed by who I am right now. Because I'm so wound up and exhausted all the time, I've gotten incredibly tense and irritable. I'll be out on the field and someone will miss a pass or make a stupid joke, and I'll just feel this massive flash of anger and snap at them. Or we'll be sitting around after the game, and I can't even participate in the conversation because I'm sitting there bouncing my leg, secretly doing mortgage math in my head, totally checked out. I just feel like I'm horrible to be around right now, so it's easier to just avoid them. To avoid everything. But then, of course, I sit at home alone and worry about the fact that I'm alienating all my friends, which just makes the whole thing worse. It's just this endless loop.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

It's just this endless loop.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever experienced sudden episodes where you feel intense fear or discomfort that peaks within minutes, along with physical symptoms like shortness of breath, chest pain, or dizziness?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 47 / computer_or_information_systems_manager / bachelors\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Sudden... no. Not sudden, and not really peaking like that. I mean, I know what you're asking about, like those moments where people feel like they're having a heart attack or can't catch their breath, right? I don't get that. It's actually kind of the opposite of sudden for me. It's not a spike, it's a plateau. A really, really high plateau that I just live on 24/7 now. 

I don't get dizzy, and I don't lose my breath. My chest... well, I guess my shoulders and chest get tight, but that's just because I'm literally clenching my muscles all day long from the stress. Like I was telling you with the soccer stuff, I'm just so physically tense all the time. But it's not a sudden pain or anything like that. It's not like I'm fine one minute and then suddenly gasping for air the next. 

If I had to describe it in IT terms—because that's just how my brain works—it's not a sudden system crash where everything just goes black and all the alarms start blaring all at once. It's more like a memory leak. It just slowly, continuously consumes all my processing power in the background until the whole system is just dragging, barely functioning, running at max capacity, but it never actually reboots. It just grinds away, constantly. I wake up, and the dread is already just sitting there, and it stays with me while I make my coffee, while I check my emails... it never really peaks because it never really drops, if that makes sense. So, no, I don't have those sudden episodes with the dizziness or the breathing issues. It's just a constant, heavy dread that never really gives me a break to even know what a sudden peak would feel like.

Like, say a client isn't responding to a routine email. Instead of just assuming they stepped away from their desk or went to lunch, within minutes I’m entirely convinced that they're furious, and that I’m going to lose the contract. And then, because I can't stop the train of thought, I start calculating how losing that contract means my department takes a hit, which means I'm going to fall behind on my divorce payments. And if I mess up those payments, my ex is going to drag me back to court, my finances will be completely ruined, and with that kind of hit to my life and reputation, I'll just never get back to a real leadership role again. \n\nI worked hard to get my degree and work my way up to management, and I just sit there watching my entire life collapse in my head over one unreturned email. But it feels so incredibly real in the moment, and I just sit there spiraling, going over every catastrophic detail. It just goes on and on like that, and I can't seem to make it stop.\n\nDoctor: Do these anxious thoughts happen mostly in social situations, or do they come up even when you're alone or dealing with routine tasks on your own?\n\nPatient: Oh, it’s definitely not just social situations. In fact, being alone with routine tasks is sometimes the absolute worst time for it, because there's absolutely no buffer, you know? I don't have to keep up a professional face, so the thoughts just run completely wild. I could be sitting completely by myself in my home office, just running a standard system update or checking server logs—something I've done a million times—and my mind keeps jumping from one catastrophic scenario to the next. \n\nIt never just stays on the task in front of me. I’ll be looking at a screen, and suddenly I'm worrying about my current contract work, and then my brain just immediately leaps over to my mortgage payments and whether I'm going to default. And then... well, then it gets into this whole other layer where I start agonizing over my reputation. I'll sit there alone and start obsessing over whether people in the Bethesda tech circles think I’m damaged goods now because of the divorce or if they think I'm losing my edge. \n\nIt’s just this constant, exhausting chain reaction. Like, just last week I was at home, by myself, waiting on what should have been a very standard, routine email from a recruiter. Nothing crazy. But it was delayed. And instead of just thinking, 'Oh, they're in a meeting,' I sat there in my kitchen and spiraled to the point where that one delayed email felt like absolute, undeniable proof that my whole career is closing down. I literally convinced myself my professional life was over, just sitting there alone with my laptop. So, yeah, I don't need to be at a networking event or a crowded meeting for this to happen. The worry just finds me wherever I am, whatever I'm doing, and it just bounces from my finances to my career to my reputation without giving me a single second to breathe.\n\nDoctor: When these anxious thoughts come up, do you also notice any physical symptoms, like restlessness, trouble sleeping, muscle tension, or a racing heart?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. It's not just in my head, it's... it's like my whole body is involved in this, you know? The tension is just constant. I feel completely wound up almost all day long. My shoulders, for instance—they are just locked tight up around my ears from the minute I wake up. It’s like I’m physically bracing myself, just constantly ready for some terrible news to drop, even when there's absolutely no reason to expect any. \n\nAnd the restlessness, it’s just... it's exhausting. I can't just sit still anymore. If I'm on a conference call—even a totally routine one where I’m just listening in and nothing is actually happening—I’ll be sitting there just bouncing my leg up and down like a jackhammer under the desk. Or if I'm off camera, I'll literally just pace back and forth across my home office. I'll walk from the desk to the window, back to the desk, into the hallway, just pacing and pacing while I'm trying to listen to server updates or whatever. \n\nIt’s like there’s this electric current just running through me all the time and I have to move to bleed some of it off, but it never really goes away. I just feel so physically keyed up, like a spring wound way too tight, and no matter how much I pace around the house or try to stretch out my neck and shoulders, that locked-in, braced feeling just stays right there. It's like my body is reacting to an emergency that isn't even happening, and I just can't get it to stand down.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you worry excessively about a variety of things in your life, even when there's no clear reason to be concerned, and does it feel like this worry is hard to control or stop, even when you try?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... yes, absolutely. It's exactly like that. It's just this constant, unending barrage of things to worry about, and I really, truly cannot control it. I try, I really do. I'll tell myself, 'Okay, just focus on this one task,' but it never works. It's like my brain has a mind of its own, if that makes sense. \n\nLike I was saying before, it's never just one thing. My mind keeps jumping from my current contract work, which is stressful enough on its own, right? But then it doesn't stop there. It immediately leaps over to my mortgage payments, and I start doing all this math in my head about what happens if a contract falls through and I can't make the payment, and then I'm picturing foreclosure. And then, as if that's not enough, it pivots entirely to my reputation. I'll just sit there obsessing over whether people in the Bethesda tech circles—which is a really tight-knit community, everybody talks, you know?—I'll worry that they think I’m damaged goods now because of the messy divorce, or that they think I'm losing my edge because I'm distracted. \n\nIt just bounces from one disaster to the next. And there doesn't even need to be a real reason for it! Like that recruiter email I mentioned. It was just delayed. Probably the guy was just at lunch or stuck on a call. But to me, in that moment? That delayed email felt like absolute, undeniable proof that my whole career is closing down. I sat there and basically planned out my entire professional funeral just because an email was an hour late. And I know how irrational that sounds when I say it out loud to you, I really do. I'm a logical guy, I work in IT, I deal with facts and systems. But when the worry hits, all that logic just goes right out the window, and I can't stop the spiral no matter how hard I try to reason with myself.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this constant and uncontrollable worry, and was there a specific event or change in your life that seemed to trigger it?\n\nPatient: Well, if I really trace it back... it’s been a little over a year now. Thirteen months, to be exact. Thirteen months of just... this. That was when I got let go from my old firm. I was fired, and honestly, that was the absolute catalyst for all of this. I had been there for a long time, you know? I had built up my department, I knew the systems inside and out, and then suddenly—just, bam, I'm out. The floor completely dropped out from under me. \n\nAt first, I thought the constant worrying was just a normal reaction to losing my job. I mean, anyone would be stressed about that, right? You lose your primary income, you lose your daily routine, you immediately start doing the math on how many months you can survive before the savings run dry. I remember sitting at my kitchen table the morning after it happened, just staring at my laptop, trying to figure out how to even begin updating my resume after all those years, and that's when this... this engine of worry just turned on in my head. \n\nBut the craziest part, and the part that really exhausts me, is that I thought it would stop once I got back on my feet. I really, truly believed that. I told myself, 'Okay, I just need to find a new gig, get some income flowing again, and my brain will finally settle down.' But I did find work. I transitioned over to doing contract work, managing some system migrations on a freelance basis, and it keeps the lights on. But the worry? It never let up. Not even a fraction of a bit. It didn't go away when I signed that first contract. \n\nIf anything, it just shifted its focus. Instead of worrying about not having a job at all, now I'm obsessing over the contract ending early, or the client suddenly deciding they hate my work, or an invoice getting held up in accounting and causing me to miss a mortgage payment. It's like being fired thirteen months ago just permanently broke some kind of switch in my brain, and now it's stuck in the 'on' position no matter what my actual employment status is. Even with the contract work coming in, the dread is just exactly the same as it was the day I lost my job. I just can't seem to turn it off.\n\nDoctor: When you start feeling anxious or overwhelmed by these thoughts, do you find yourself actively avoiding certain situations, tasks, or even people because you think they might trigger more of these worries?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. All the time now. It's actually becoming a huge problem, both professionally and personally, and I just... I don't know how to break out of it. \n\nLike with the contract work I was just telling you about... I'll have a deliverable due, right? Something standard, like a system architecture review or a migration plan. I know how to do it, I've done a million of them in my career. But I'll sit down to send the final file, and my brain just starts screaming at me. 'What if you missed a critical vulnerability? What if the client hates the formatting? What if they think your work is sloppy and they tell everyone in the Bethesda tech loop?' So... I just don't send it. I'll tell myself I just need to review it one more time tomorrow. And then tomorrow comes, and I do the exact same thing. I’ve literally delayed client deliverables for days because I just get completely stuck, paralyzed really, anticipating every single little thing that could possibly go wrong. \n\nAnd networking? Forget about it. In this freelance game, you have to network, but I've missed so many crucial follow-ups. I'll get someone's contact info, and then I'll just stare at a blank email draft for an hour, convinced that whatever I write is going to make me sound desperate or incompetent, until I just close the window and avoid them entirely. \n\nAnd the worst part is that it’s not just work. It’s bleeding into everything else. I used to play in this adult rec soccer league. Nothing crazy, just a bunch of guys trying not to pull a hamstring on Thursday nights, you know? Because I'm so wound up and exhausted all the time, I've gotten incredibly tense and irritable. I'll be out on the field and someone will miss a pass or make a stupid joke, and I'll just feel this massive flash of anger and snap at them. Or we'll be sitting around after the game, and I can't even participate in the conversation because I'm sitting there bouncing my leg, secretly doing mortgage math in my head, totally checked out. I just feel like I'm horrible to be around right now, so it's easier to just avoid them. To avoid everything. It's just this endless loop.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced sudden episodes where you feel intense fear or discomfort that peaks within minutes, along with physical symptoms like shortness of breath, chest pain, or dizziness?\n\nPatient: Sudden... no. Well, no, not really. I mean, I know I just said I stopped going to my soccer games, and I guess from the outside that probably looks exactly like losing interest, right? Like someone who’s just given up on their hobbies. But it’s really not that at all. I still want to play. I miss playing. I miss the guys. If I could just magically turn off this buzzing in my head, I’d be out on that field in a heartbeat. So it’s definitely not a lack of interest... it’s more like a surplus of panic. The interest is still there, it’s just completely buried under all the stress and the tension I was telling you about. 

And as for feeling down, or like life isn't worth living... no. Definitely not that. I don't have those kinds of dark thoughts about giving up or not wanting to be here. Honestly, it’s almost the exact opposite. If I didn't care, or if I thought life wasn't worth it, I wouldn't be spending twenty-four hours a day absolutely terrified of losing everything, you know? I’m fighting tooth and nail just to keep my head above water with these freelance contracts and the mortgage. You don't do that if you’ve given up. You’d just let the water take you. But I’m treading water so hard I’m exhausting myself. 

I mean, sure, I get frustrated. I get incredibly frustrated and demoralized that my brain is stuck in this loop, and I hate how tense I am all the time, but I don't feel worthless. I know I have value. I know I have skills—I built an entire IT department from the ground up at my last firm, I know what I'm capable of when my brain isn't sabotaging me. So, no, it's not a depression thing. I don't feel hopeless about life itself, I just feel completely hijacked by this constant, grinding worry about what's going to go wrong next.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n8\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F43.22\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hi, yeah, thank you for fitting me in. I'm... well, I came in because I just can’t shut my brain off anymore. It’s like the off switch is completely broken. I work as an IT systems manager, so I’m used to tracking a lot of moving parts and anticipating problems, but lately, my mind just takes off on its own and I absolutely cannot stop it. \n\nIt usually starts with just one tiny, insignificant concern, you know? Like, say a client isn't responding to a routine email. Instead of just assuming they stepped away from their desk or went to lunch, within minutes I’m entirely convinced that they're furious, and that I’m going to lose the contract. And then, because I can't stop the train of thought, I start calculating how losing that contract means my department takes a hit, which means I'm going to fall behind on my divorce payments. And if I mess up those payments, my ex is going to drag me back to court, my finances will be completely ruined, and with that kind of hit to my life and reputation, I'll just never get back to a real leadership role again. \n\nI worked hard to get my degree and work my way up to management, and I just sit there watching my entire life collapse in my head over one unreturned email. But it feels so incredibly real in the moment, and I just sit there spiraling, going over every catastrophic detail. It just goes on and on like that, and I can't seem to make it stop.\n\nDoctor: Do these anxious thoughts happen mostly in social situations, or do they come up even when you're alone or dealing with routine tasks on your own?\n\nPatient: Oh, it’s definitely not just social situations. In fact, being alone with routine tasks is sometimes the absolute worst time for it, because there's absolutely no buffer, you know? I don't have to keep up a professional face, so the thoughts just run completely wild. I could be sitting completely by myself in my home office, just running a standard system update or checking server logs—something I've done a million times—and my mind keeps jumping from one catastrophic scenario to the next. \n\nIt never just stays on the task in front of me. I’ll be looking at a screen, and suddenly I'm worrying about my current contract work, and then my brain just immediately leaps over to my mortgage payments and whether I'm going to default. And then... well, then it gets into this whole other layer where I start agonizing over my reputation. I'll sit there alone and start obsessing over whether people in the Bethesda tech circles think I’m damaged goods now because of the divorce or if they think I'm losing my edge. \n\nIt’s just this constant, exhausting chain reaction. Like, just last week I was at home, by myself, waiting on what should have been a very standard, routine email from a recruiter. Nothing crazy. But it was delayed. And instead of just thinking, 'Oh, they're in a meeting,' I sat there in my kitchen and spiraled to the point where that one delayed email felt like absolute, undeniable proof that my whole career is closing down. I literally convinced myself my professional life was over, just sitting there alone with my laptop. So, yeah, I don't need to be at a networking event or a crowded meeting for this to happen. The worry just finds me wherever I am, whatever I'm doing, and it just bounces from my finances to my career to my reputation without giving me a single second to breathe.\n\nDoctor: When these anxious thoughts come up, do you also notice any physical symptoms, like restlessness, trouble sleeping, muscle tension, or a racing heart?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. It's not just in my head, it's... it's like my whole body is involved in this, you know? The tension is just constant. I feel completely wound up almost all day long. My shoulders, for instance—they are just locked tight up around my ears from the minute I wake up. It’s like I’m physically bracing myself, just constantly ready for some terrible news to drop, even when there's absolutely no reason to expect any. \n\nAnd the restlessness, it’s just... it's exhausting. I can't just sit still anymore. If I'm on a conference call—even a totally routine one where I’m just listening in and nothing is actually happening—I’ll be sitting there just bouncing my leg up and down like a jackhammer under the desk. Or if I'm off camera, I'll literally just pace back and forth across my home office. I'll walk from the desk to the window, back to the desk, into the hallway, just pacing and pacing while I'm trying to listen to server updates or whatever. \n\nIt’s like there’s this electric current just running through me all the time and I have to move to bleed some of it off, but it never really goes away. I just feel so physically keyed up, like a spring wound way too tight, and no matter how much I pace around the house or try to stretch out my neck and shoulders, that locked-in, braced feeling just stays right there. It's like my body is reacting to an emergency that isn't even happening, and I just can't get it to stand down.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you worry excessively about a variety of things in your life, even when there's no clear reason to be concerned, and does it feel like this worry is hard to control or stop, even when you try?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... yes, absolutely. It's exactly like that. It's just this constant, unending barrage of things to worry about, and I really, truly cannot control it. I try, I really do. I'll tell myself, 'Okay, just focus on this one task,' but it never works. It's like my brain has a mind of its own, if that makes sense. \n\nLike I was saying before, it's never just one thing. My mind keeps jumping from my current contract work, which is stressful enough on its own, right? But then it doesn't stop there. It immediately leaps over to my mortgage payments, and I start doing all this math in my head about what happens if a contract falls through and I can't make the payment, and then I'm picturing foreclosure. And then, as if that's not enough, it pivots entirely to my reputation. I'll just sit there obsessing over whether people in the Bethesda tech circles—which is a really tight-knit community, everybody talks, you know?—I'll worry that they think I’m damaged goods now because of the messy divorce, or that they think I'm losing my edge because I'm distracted. \n\nIt just bounces from one disaster to the next. And there doesn't even need to be a real reason for it! Like that recruiter email I mentioned. It was just delayed. Probably the guy was just at lunch or stuck on a call. But to me, in that moment? That delayed email felt like absolute, undeniable proof that my whole career is closing down. I sat there and basically planned out my entire professional funeral just because an email was an hour late. And I know how irrational that sounds when I say it out loud to you, I really do. I'm a logical guy, I work in IT, I deal with facts and systems. But when the worry hits, all that logic just goes right out the window, and I can't stop the spiral no matter how hard I try to reason with myself.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this constant and uncontrollable worry, and was there a specific event or change in your life that seemed to trigger it?\n\nPatient: Well, if I really trace it back... it’s been a little over a year now. Thirteen months, to be exact. Thirteen months of just... this. That was when I got let go from my old firm. I was fired, and honestly, that was the absolute catalyst for all of this. I had been there for a long time, you know? I had built up my department, I knew the systems inside and out, and then suddenly—just, bam, I'm out. The floor completely dropped out from under me. \n\nAt first, I thought the constant worrying was just a normal reaction to losing my job. I mean, anyone would be stressed about that, right? You lose your primary income, you lose your daily routine, you immediately start doing the math on how many months you can survive before the savings run dry. I remember sitting at my kitchen table the morning after it happened, just staring at my laptop, trying to figure out how to even begin updating my resume after all those years, and that's when this... this engine of worry just turned on in my head. \n\nBut the craziest part, and the part that really exhausts me, is that I thought it would stop once I got back on my feet. I really, truly believed that. I told myself, 'Okay, I just need to find a new gig, get some income flowing again, and my brain will finally settle down.' But I did find work. I transitioned over to doing contract work, managing some system migrations on a freelance basis, and it keeps the lights on. But the worry? It never let up. Not even a fraction of a bit. It didn't go away when I signed that first contract. \n\nIf anything, it just shifted its focus. Instead of worrying about not having a job at all, now I'm obsessing over the contract ending early, or the client suddenly deciding they hate my work, or an invoice getting held up in accounting and causing me to miss a mortgage payment. It's like being fired thirteen months ago just permanently broke some kind of switch in my brain, and now it's stuck in the 'on' position no matter what my actual employment status is. Even with the contract work coming in, the dread is just exactly the same as it was the day I lost my job. I just can't seem to turn it off.\n\nDoctor: When you start feeling anxious or overwhelmed by these thoughts, do you find yourself actively avoiding certain situations, tasks, or even people because you think they might trigger more of these worries?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. All the time now. It's actually becoming a huge problem, both professionally and personally, and I just... I don't know how to break out of it. \n\nLike with the contract work I was just telling you about... I'll have a deliverable due, right? Something standard, like a system architecture review or a migration plan. I know how to do it, I've done a million of them in my career. But I'll sit down to send the final file, and my brain just starts screaming at me. 'What if you missed a critical vulnerability? What if the client hates the formatting? What if they think your work is sloppy and they tell everyone in the Bethesda tech loop?' So... I just don't send it. I'll tell myself I just need to review it one more time tomorrow. And then tomorrow comes, and I do the exact same thing. I’ve literally delayed client deliverables for days because I just get completely stuck, paralyzed really, anticipating every single little thing that could possibly go wrong. \n\nAnd networking? Forget about it. In this freelance game, you have to network, but I've missed so many crucial follow-ups. I'll get someone's contact info, and then I'll just stare at a blank email draft for an hour, convinced that whatever I write is going to make me sound desperate or incompetent, until I just close the window and avoid them entirely. \n\nAnd the worst part is that it’s not just work. It’s bleeding into everything else. I used to play in this adult rec soccer league. Nothing crazy, just a bunch of guys trying not to pull a hamstring on Thursday nights, you know? We’d play, maybe grab a beer after. I've played with some of these guys for years. But I’ve completely pulled back from that. I haven't gone in over a month, and I've basically ghosted my friends there. \n\nAnd honestly... it's because I’m just so embarrassed. I'm embarrassed by who I am right now. Because I'm so wound up and exhausted all the time, I've gotten incredibly tense and irritable. I'll be out on the field and someone will miss a pass or make a stupid joke, and I'll just feel this massive flash of anger and snap at them. Or we'll be sitting around after the game, and I can't even participate in the conversation because I'm sitting there bouncing my leg, secretly doing mortgage math in my head, totally checked out. I just feel like I'm horrible to be around right now, so it's easier to just avoid them. To avoid everything. But then, of course, I sit at home alone and worry about the fact that I'm alienating all my friends, which just makes the whole thing worse. It's just this endless loop.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced sudden episodes where you feel intense fear or discomfort that peaks within minutes, along with physical symptoms like shortness of breath, chest pain, or dizziness?\n\nPatient: Sudden... no. Not sudden, and not really peaking like that. I mean, I know what you're asking about, like those moments where people feel like they're having a heart attack or can't catch their breath, right? I don't get that. It's actually kind of the opposite of sudden for me. It's not a spike, it's a plateau. A really, really high plateau that I just live on 24/7 now. \n\nI don't get dizzy, and I don't lose my breath. My chest... well, I guess my shoulders and chest get tight, but that's just because I'm literally clenching my muscles all day long from the stress. Like I was telling you with the soccer stuff, I'm just so physically tense all the time. But it's not a sudden pain or anything like that. It's not like I'm fine one minute and then suddenly gasping for air the next. \n\nIf I had to describe it in IT terms—because that's just how my brain works—it's not a sudden system crash where everything just goes black and all the alarms start blaring all at once. It's more like a memory leak. It just slowly, continuously consumes all my processing power in the background until the whole system is just dragging, barely functioning, running at max capacity, but it never actually reboots. It just grinds away, constantly. I wake up, and the dread is already just sitting there, and it stays with me while I make my coffee, while I check my emails... it never really peaks because it never really drops, if that makes sense. So, no, I don't have those sudden episodes with the dizziness or the breathing issues. It's just a constant, heavy dread that never really gives me a break to even know what a sudden peak would feel like.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself feeling down, losing interest in things you used to enjoy, or feeling like you're not good enough or that life isn't worth living?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I mean, I know I just said I stopped going to my soccer games, and I guess from the outside that probably looks exactly like losing interest, right? Like someone who’s just given up on their hobbies. But it’s really not that at all. I still want to play. I miss playing. I miss the guys. If I could just magically turn off this buzzing in my head, I’d be out on that field in a heartbeat. So it’s definitely not a lack of interest... it’s more like a surplus of panic. The interest is still there, it’s just completely buried under all the stress and the tension I was telling you about. \n\nAnd as for feeling down, or like life isn't worth living... no. Definitely not that. I don't have those kinds of dark thoughts about giving up or not wanting to be here. Honestly, it’s almost the exact opposite. If I didn't care, or if I thought life wasn't worth it, I wouldn't be spending twenty-four hours a day absolutely terrified of losing everything, you know? I’m fighting tooth and nail just to keep my head above water with these freelance contracts and the mortgage. You don't do that if you’ve given up. You’d just let the water take you. But I’m treading water so hard I’m exhausting myself. \n\nI mean, sure, I get frustrated. I get incredibly frustrated and demoralized that my brain is stuck in this loop, and I hate how tense I am all the time, but I don't feel worthless. I know I have value. I know I have skills—I built an entire IT department from the ground up at my last firm, I know what I'm capable of when my brain isn't sabotaging me. So, no, it's not a depression thing. I don't feel hopeless about life itself, I just feel completely hijacked by this constant, grinding worry about what's going to go wrong next.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

Concentrate? No, I don't... I don't think so. I mean, I guess it depends on what you mean by focus, but if we're talking about just the sheer ability to lock in on something? No, I haven't noticed any problems with that at all. Actually, it’s kind of the exact opposite for me. 

When you're managing information systems, especially when you're doing the kind of high-level architecture reviews and migrations I do, your entire job is concentration. You have to be able to hold this massive, complex web of dependencies in your head all at once. And I can still do that. I can sit down at my desk—my home office, which I spent weeks setting up perfectly with the dual monitors and the standing desk, because if I'm going to be doing this freelance thing I need the environment to be totally optimized—but anyway, I'll sit there and I don't lose focus at all. I will stare at a network topology map, tracing every single data path, looking for vulnerabilities, for hours. Literally hours without blinking, without getting up to get a glass of water, without even noticing that the sun went down outside. 

So the focus is definitely there. My brain isn't jumping around, you know? It's not like I'm easily distracted by a bird outside the window or a notification on my phone. It's just that the worry... it doesn't break my concentration, it kind of fuses with it. I get hyper-focused. Like, I'll be looking at a standard firewall configuration, and the thought will pop into my head: 'What if there's a zero-day exploit in this specific firmware version and the client gets ransomed six months from now and blames me?' And instead of losing my train of thought, I just drill down into that one specific, incredibly unlikely scenario with absolute laser precision. I'll spend three hours researching that one tiny 'what if' until my eyes are burning. 

So I'm locked in. I'm completely, one-hundred-percent locked in. I can concentrate just fine. I just can't seem to concentrate on anything *but* the absolute worst-case scenarios. I'm focusing so hard on trying to prevent the disaster that I can't step back and just finish the basic task. Honestly, I kind of wish I *did* find it hard to concentrate sometimes. I wish I could just get distracted and lose my focus, just to get a five-minute break from my own brain, to be completely honest with you. But no, staying focused isn't the issue at all.

I just feel like I'm horrible to be around right now, so it's easier to just avoid them. To avoid everything. It's just this endless loop.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced sudden episodes where you feel intense fear or discomfort that peaks within minutes, along with physical symptoms like shortness of breath, chest pain, or dizziness?\n\nPatient: Sudden... no. I don't get that. It's actually kind of the opposite of sudden for me. It's not a spike, it's a plateau. It's not like I'm fine one minute and then suddenly gasping for air the next. \n\nIf I had to describe it in IT terms—because that's just how my brain works—it's not a sudden system crash where everything just goes black and all the alarms start blaring all at once. It just grinds away, constantly.
