A really, really high plateau that I just live on 24/7 now. \n\nI don't get dizzy, and I don't lose my breath. My chest... well, I guess my shoulders and chest get tight, but that's just because I'm literally clenching my muscles all day long from the stress. Like I was telling you with the soccer stuff, I'm just so physically tense all the time. But it's not a sudden pain or anything like that. It's not like I'm fine one minute and then suddenly gasping for air the next. \n\nIf I had to describe it in IT terms—because that's just how my brain works—it's not a sudden system crash where everything just goes black and all the alarms start blaring all at once. It's more like a memory leak. It just slowly, continuously consumes all my processing power in the background until the whole system is just dragging, barely functioning, running at max capacity, but it never actually reboots. It just grinds away, constantly. I wake up, and the dread is already just sitting there, and it stays with me while I make my coffee, while I check my emails... it never really peaks because it never really drops, if that makes sense. So, no, I don't have those sudden episodes with the dizziness or the breathing issues. It's just a constant, heavy dread that never really gives me a break to even know what a sudden peak would feel like.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself feeling down, losing interest in things you used to enjoy, or feeling like you're not good enough or that life isn't worth living?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I mean, I know I just said I stopped going to my soccer games, and I guess from the outside that probably looks exactly like losing interest, right? Like someone who’s just given up on their hobbies. But it’s really not that at all. I still want to play. I miss playing. I miss the guys. If I could just magically turn off this buzzing in my head, I’d be out on that field in a heartbeat. So it’s definitely not a lack of interest... it’s more like a surplus of panic. The interest is still there, it’s just completely buried under all the stress and the tension I was telling you about. \n\nAnd as for feeling down, or like life isn't worth living... no. Definitely not that. I don't have those kinds of dark thoughts about giving up or not wanting to be here. Honestly, it’s almost the exact opposite. If I didn't care, or if I thought life wasn't worth it, I wouldn't be spending twenty-four hours a day absolutely terrified of losing everything, you know? I’m fighting tooth and nail just to keep my head above water with these freelance contracts and the mortgage. You don't do that if you’ve given up. You’d just let the water take you. But I’m treading water so hard I’m exhausting myself. \n\nI mean, sure, I get frustrated. I get incredibly frustrated and demoralized that my brain is stuck in this loop, and I hate how tense I am all the time, but I don't feel worthless. I know I have value. I know I have skills—I built an entire IT department from the ground up at my last firm, I know what I'm capable of when my brain isn't sabotaging me. So, no, it's not a depression thing. I don't feel hopeless about life itself, I just feel completely hijacked by this constant, grinding worry about what's going to go wrong next.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to concentrate or stay focused because your mind is so consumed with worry?\n\nPatient: Concentrate? No, I don't... I don't think so. I mean, I guess it depends on what you mean by focus, but if we're talking about just the sheer ability to lock in on something? No, I haven't noticed any problems with that at all. Actually, it’s kind of the exact opposite for me. \n\nWhen you're managing information systems, especially when you're doing the kind of high-level architecture reviews and migrations I do, your entire job is concentration. You have to be able to hold this massive, complex web of dependencies in your head all at once. And I can still do that. I can sit down at my desk—my home office, which I spent weeks setting up perfectly with the dual monitors and the standing desk, because if I'm going to be doing this freelance thing I need the environment to be totally optimized—but anyway, I'll sit there and I don't lose focus at all. I will stare at a network topology map, tracing every single data path, looking for vulnerabilities, for hours. Literally hours without blinking, without getting up to get a glass of water, without even noticing that the sun went down outside. \n\nSo the focus is definitely there. My brain isn't jumping around, you know? It's not like I'm easily distracted by a bird outside the window or a notification on my phone. It's just that the worry... it doesn't break my concentration, it kind of fuses with it. I get hyper-focused. Like, I'll be looking at a standard firewall configuration, and the thought will pop into my head: 'What if there's a zero-day exploit in this specific firmware version and the client gets ransomed six months from now and blames me?' And instead of losing my train of thought, I just drill down into that one specific, incredibly unlikely scenario with absolute laser precision. I'll spend three hours researching that one tiny 'what if' until my eyes are burning. \n\nSo I'm locked in. I'm completely, one-hundred-percent locked in. I can concentrate just fine. I just can't seem to concentrate on anything *but* the absolute worst-case scenarios. I'm focusing so hard on trying to prevent the disaster that I can't step back and just finish the basic task. Honestly, I kind of wish I *did* find it hard to concentrate sometimes. I wish I could just get distracted and lose my focus, just to get a five-minute break from my own brain, to be completely honest with you. But no, staying focused isn't the issue at all.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever found yourself excessively worrying about your physical health or specific bodily symptoms, even when there’s no clear medical cause for concern?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. My physical health? No... no, not really. I mean, I know some people get really hung up on every little ache and pain, going down those WebMD rabbit holes at two in the morning, but that’s just not me. 

Like I was saying earlier, I definitely have physical stuff going on—the tight shoulders, the chest tension—but I know exactly what that is. I don't sit around thinking I’m having a heart attack or that I have some rare neurological disease. I know it's just the stress. It’s just my muscles being locked up from sitting at my desk for fourteen hours a day, clenching my jaw while I try to keep all these freelance plates spinning. If my stomach hurts, I just figure I drank too much coffee. If my head aches, I know it's because I've been staring at dual monitors since 6:00 AM. 

Honestly, I had my annual physical back in... I want to say October? Yeah, October, right around the time I was doing this massive server migration for a client that ended up being a total nightmare. But my regular doctor ran all the standard blood panels, checked my cholesterol, my blood pressure—which was a little elevated, but nothing crazy—and everything was fine. And I just accepted that. I didn't second-guess him or demand an MRI or anything. 

If I'm being completely honest, I feel like I don't even have the bandwidth to worry about my health. It’s like my brain has allocated one hundred percent of its resources to worrying about my career, the mortgage, the contracts, and the fear of making some catastrophic error in a client's infrastructure. There is literally no RAM left over to worry about a weird mole or a cough. My wife was actually just saying the other day that I need to get my eyes checked because I've been squinting at my screens, and I just told her, "Look, if my vision goes, it goes, but right now I have to make sure this AWS deployment doesn't fail." Obviously, I don't want to lose my vision, but you get what I mean. My physical health just doesn't even register on my dashboard of imminent catastrophes. All the alarms in my head are strictly tied to my work and my finances, so no, I don't really obsess over my body or get anxious about medical stuff at all. It's just not where my worry lives.

A really, really high plateau that I just live on 24/7 now. \n\nI don't get dizzy, and I don't lose my breath. My chest... well, I guess my shoulders and chest get tight, but that's just because I'm literally clenching my muscles all day long from the stress. Like I was telling you with the soccer stuff, I'm just so physically tense all the time. But it's not a sudden pain or anything like that. It's not like I'm fine one minute and then suddenly gasping for air the next. \n\nIf I had to describe it in IT terms—because that's just how my brain works—it's not a sudden system crash where everything just goes black and all the alarms start blaring all at once. It's more like a memory leak. It just slowly, continuously consumes all my processing power in the background until the whole system is just dragging, barely functioning, running at max capacity, but it never actually reboots. It just grinds away, constantly. I wake up, and the dread is already just sitting there, and it stays with me while I make my coffee, while I check my emails... it never really peaks because it never really drops, if that makes sense. So, no, I don't have those sudden episodes with the dizziness or the breathing issues. It's just a constant, heavy dread that never really gives me a break to even know what a sudden peak would feel like.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself feeling down, losing interest in things you used to enjoy, or feeling like you're not good enough or that life isn't worth living?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I mean, I know I just said I stopped going to my soccer games, and I guess from the outside that probably looks exactly like losing interest, right? Like someone who’s just given up on their hobbies. But it’s really not that at all. I still want to play. I miss playing. I miss the guys. If I could just magically turn off this buzzing in my head, I’d be out on that field in a heartbeat. So it’s definitely not a lack of interest... it’s more like a surplus of panic. The interest is still there, it’s just completely buried under all the stress and the tension I was telling you about. \n\nAnd as for feeling down, or like life isn't worth living... no. Definitely not that. I don't have those kinds of dark thoughts about giving up or not wanting to be here. Honestly, it’s almost the exact opposite. If I didn't care, or if I thought life wasn't worth it, I wouldn't be spending twenty-four hours a day absolutely terrified of losing everything, you know? I’m fighting tooth and nail just to keep my head above water with these freelance contracts and the mortgage. You don't do that if you’ve given up. You’d just let the water take you. But I’m treading water so hard I’m exhausting myself. \n\nI mean, sure, I get frustrated. I get incredibly frustrated and demoralized that my brain is stuck in this loop, and I hate how tense I am all the time, but I don't feel worthless. I know I have value. I know I have skills—I built an entire IT department from the ground up at my last firm, I know what I'm capable of when my brain isn't sabotaging me. So, no, it's not a depression thing. I don't feel hopeless about life itself, I just feel completely hijacked by this constant, grinding worry about what's going to go wrong next.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to concentrate or stay focused because your mind is so consumed with worry?\n\nPatient: Concentrate? No, I don't... I don't think so. I mean, I guess it depends on what you mean by focus, but if we're talking about just the sheer ability to lock in on something? No, I haven't noticed any problems with that at all. Actually, it’s kind of the exact opposite for me. \n\nWhen you're managing information systems, especially when you're doing the kind of high-level architecture reviews and migrations I do, your entire job is concentration. You have to be able to hold this massive, complex web of dependencies in your head all at once. And I can still do that. I can sit down at my desk—my home office, which I spent weeks setting up perfectly with the dual monitors and the standing desk, because if I'm going to be doing this freelance thing I need the environment to be totally optimized—but anyway, I'll sit there and I don't lose focus at all. I will stare at a network topology map, tracing every single data path, looking for vulnerabilities, for hours. Literally hours without blinking, without getting up to get a glass of water, without even noticing that the sun went down outside. \n\nSo the focus is definitely there. My brain isn't jumping around, you know? It's not like I'm easily distracted by a bird outside the window or a notification on my phone. It's just that the worry... it doesn't break my concentration, it kind of fuses with it. I get hyper-focused. Like, I'll be looking at a standard firewall configuration, and the thought will pop into my head: 'What if there's a zero-day exploit in this specific firmware version and the client gets ransomed six months from now and blames me?' And instead of losing my train of thought, I just drill down into that one specific, incredibly unlikely scenario with absolute laser precision. I'll spend three hours researching that one tiny 'what if' until my eyes are burning. \n\nSo I'm locked in. I'm completely, one-hundred-percent locked in. I can concentrate just fine. I just can't seem to concentrate on anything *but* the absolute worst-case scenarios. I'm focusing so hard on trying to prevent the disaster that I can't step back and just finish the basic task. Honestly, I kind of wish I *did* find it hard to concentrate sometimes. I wish I could just get distracted and lose my focus, just to get a five-minute break from my own brain, to be completely honest with you. I don't sit around thinking I’m having a heart attack or that I have some rare neurological disease. I know it's just the stress. It’s just my muscles being locked up from sitting at my desk for fourteen hours a day, clenching my jaw while I try to keep all these freelance plates spinning. If my stomach hurts, I just figure I drank too much coffee. If my head aches, I know it's because I've been staring at dual monitors since 6:00 AM. \n\nHonestly, I had my annual physical back in... I didn't second-guess him or demand an MRI or anything. \n\nIf I'm being completely honest, I feel like I don't even have the bandwidth to worry about my health. My wife was actually just saying the other day that I need to get my eyes checked because I've been squinting at my screens, and I just told her, \"Look, if my vision goes, it goes, but right now I have to make sure this AWS deployment doesn't fail.\" Obviously, I don't want to lose my vision, but you get what I mean. My physical health just doesn't even register on my dashboard of imminent catastrophes. It's just not where my worry lives.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I don't feel hopeless about life itself, I just feel completely hijacked by this constant, grinding worry about what's going to go wrong next.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to concentrate or stay focused because your mind is so consumed with worry?\n\nPatient: Concentrate? You have to be able to hold this massive, complex web of dependencies in your head all at once. And I can still do that. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, sleep. Yeah. That’s... that’s a whole other disaster area entirely. 

Most nights, I just lie there in the dark. My wife will be dead asleep next to me, breathing perfectly normally, and I’m just staring at the ceiling fan, completely wired. My brain just decides that midnight is the perfect time to do a full, comprehensive review of everything I might have messed up recently. I'll lie there replaying interview answers from client pitches I did three days ago. I'll literally reconstruct the entire conversation in my head, analyzing every pause, every inflection. Like, did I sound too desperate when we talked about my hourly rate? Did I explain that cloud migration strategy clearly enough, or did the hiring manager think I was out of my depth? I just play the tape over and over and over again until I'm physically tense under the blankets.

And then, if my body finally just gives out and I do manage to drift off, it’s not like it's a deep, restful sleep. I’ll jolt awake, my heart will be racing, and I’ll roll over to check the clock, and it is always, without fail, 2:00 or 3:00 a.m. Every single time. And once I see that glowing number, the countdown math starts. I'll think, 'Okay, if I can just force myself back to sleep right this second, I can still get three and a half hours before the alarm.' But of course, doing that mental math just wakes me up even more, and then the worry loop about the mortgage and the freelance contracts boots right back up. 

But honestly, the most frustrating part is that even on the rare occasions where I get what should technically be enough sleep—like if I look at my phone in the morning and I was actually in bed, unconscious, for seven or eight hours—it doesn't even matter. I don't feel rested at all. I wake up feeling exactly like I spent the entire night on call for a massive system outage. 

I don't know if you've ever had to deal with that in your line of work, but in IT, when a critical server goes down at 3 a.m. and you're just running on pure adrenaline and panic, trying to patch the infrastructure before the whole company logs on at 8 a.m... you feel completely hollowed out and electrified at the same time. That’s how I feel every single morning when I open my eyes. My brain is already red-lining, I'm exhausted, and I haven't even thrown the covers off yet. So, yeah... sleep isn't really sleep for me right now. It's just a different room for me to worry in.

It’s like the off switch is completely broken. I work as an IT systems manager, so I’m used to tracking a lot of moving parts and anticipating problems, but lately, my mind just takes off on its own and I absolutely cannot stop it. \n\nIt usually starts with just one tiny, insignificant concern, you know? Like, say a client isn't responding to a routine email. Instead of just assuming they stepped away from their desk or went to lunch, within minutes I’m entirely convinced that they're furious, and that I’m going to lose the contract. And then, because I can't stop the train of thought, I start calculating how losing that contract means my department takes a hit, which means I'm going to fall behind on my divorce payments. And if I mess up those payments, my ex is going to drag me back to court, my finances will be completely ruined, and with that kind of hit to my life and reputation, I'll just never get back to a real leadership role again. \n\nI worked hard to get my degree and work my way up to management, and I just sit there watching my entire life collapse in my head over one unreturned email. But it feels so incredibly real in the moment, and I just sit there spiraling, going over every catastrophic detail. It just goes on and on like that, and I can't seem to make it stop.\n\nDoctor: Do these anxious thoughts happen mostly in social situations, or do they come up even when you're alone or dealing with routine tasks on your own?\n\nPatient: Oh, it’s definitely not just social situations. In fact, being alone with routine tasks is sometimes the absolute worst time for it, because there's absolutely no buffer, you know? I don't have to keep up a professional face, so the thoughts just run completely wild. I could be sitting completely by myself in my home office, just running a standard system update or checking server logs—something I've done a million times—and my mind keeps jumping from one catastrophic scenario to the next. \n\nIt never just stays on the task in front of me. I’ll be looking at a screen, and suddenly I'm worrying about my current contract work, and then my brain just immediately leaps over to my mortgage payments and whether I'm going to default. And then... well, then it gets into this whole other layer where I start agonizing over my reputation. I'll sit there alone and start obsessing over whether people in the Bethesda tech circles think I’m damaged goods now because of the divorce or if they think I'm losing my edge. \n\nIt’s just this constant, exhausting chain reaction. Like, just last week I was at home, by myself, waiting on what should have been a very standard, routine email from a recruiter. Nothing crazy. But it was delayed. And instead of just thinking, 'Oh, they're in a meeting,' I sat there in my kitchen and spiraled to the point where that one delayed email felt like absolute, undeniable proof that my whole career is closing down. I literally convinced myself my professional life was over, just sitting there alone with my laptop. So, yeah, I don't need to be at a networking event or a crowded meeting for this to happen. The worry just finds me wherever I am, whatever I'm doing, and it just bounces from my finances to my career to my reputation without giving me a single second to breathe.\n\nDoctor: When these anxious thoughts come up, do you also notice any physical symptoms, like restlessness, trouble sleeping, muscle tension, or a racing heart?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. It's not just in my head, it's... it's like my whole body is involved in this, you know? The tension is just constant. I feel completely wound up almost all day long. My shoulders, for instance—they are just locked tight up around my ears from the minute I wake up. It’s like I’m physically bracing myself, just constantly ready for some terrible news to drop, even when there's absolutely no reason to expect any. \n\nAnd the restlessness, it’s just... it's exhausting. I can't just sit still anymore. If I'm on a conference call—even a totally routine one where I’m just listening in and nothing is actually happening—I’ll be sitting there just bouncing my leg up and down like a jackhammer under the desk. Or if I'm off camera, I'll literally just pace back and forth across my home office. I'll walk from the desk to the window, back to the desk, into the hallway, just pacing and pacing while I'm trying to listen to server updates or whatever. \n\nIt’s like there’s this electric current just running through me all the time and I have to move to bleed some of it off, but it never really goes away. I just feel so physically keyed up, like a spring wound way too tight, and no matter how much I pace around the house or try to stretch out my neck and shoulders, that locked-in, braced feeling just stays right there. It's like my body is reacting to an emergency that isn't even happening, and I just can't get it to stand down.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you worry excessively about a variety of things in your life, even when there's no clear reason to be concerned, and does it feel like this worry is hard to control or stop, even when you try?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... yes, absolutely. It's exactly like that. It's just this constant, unending barrage of things to worry about, and I really, truly cannot control it. I try, I really do. I'll tell myself, 'Okay, just focus on this one task,' but it never works. It's like my brain has a mind of its own, if that makes sense. \n\nLike I was saying before, it's never just one thing. My mind keeps jumping from my current contract work, which is stressful enough on its own, right? But then it doesn't stop there. It immediately leaps over to my mortgage payments, and I start doing all this math in my head about what happens if a contract falls through and I can't make the payment, and then I'm picturing foreclosure. And then, as if that's not enough, it pivots entirely to my reputation. I'll just sit there obsessing over whether people in the Bethesda tech circles—which is a really tight-knit community, everybody talks, you know?—I'll worry that they think I’m damaged goods now because of the messy divorce, or that they think I'm losing my edge because I'm distracted. \n\nIt just bounces from one disaster to the next. And there doesn't even need to be a real reason for it! Like that recruiter email I mentioned. It was just delayed. Probably the guy was just at lunch or stuck on a call. But to me, in that moment? That delayed email felt like absolute, undeniable proof that my whole career is closing down. I sat there and basically planned out my entire professional funeral just because an email was an hour late. And I know how irrational that sounds when I say it out loud to you, I really do. I'm a logical guy, I work in IT, I deal with facts and systems. But when the worry hits, all that logic just goes right out the window, and I can't stop the spiral no matter how hard I try to reason with myself.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this constant and uncontrollable worry, and was there a specific event or change in your life that seemed to trigger it?\n\nPatient: Well, if I really trace it back... it’s been a little over a year now. Thirteen months, to be exact. Thirteen months of just... this. That was when I got let go from my old firm. I was fired, and honestly, that was the absolute catalyst for all of this. I had been there for a long time, you know? I had built up my department, I knew the systems inside and out, and then suddenly—just, bam, I'm out. The floor completely dropped out from under me. \n\nAt first, I thought the constant worrying was just a normal reaction to losing my job. I mean, anyone would be stressed about that, right? You lose your primary income, you lose your daily routine, you immediately start doing the math on how many months you can survive before the savings run dry. I remember sitting at my kitchen table the morning after it happened, just staring at my laptop, trying to figure out how to even begin updating my resume after all those years, and that's when this... this engine of worry just turned on in my head. \n\nBut the craziest part, and the part that really exhausts me, is that I thought it would stop once I got back on my feet. I really, truly believed that. I told myself, 'Okay, I just need to find a new gig, get some income flowing again, and my brain will finally settle down.' But I did find work. I transitioned over to doing contract work, managing some system migrations on a freelance basis, and it keeps the lights on. But the worry? It never let up. Not even a fraction of a bit. It didn't go away when I signed that first contract. \n\nIf anything, it just shifted its focus. Instead of worrying about not having a job at all, now I'm obsessing over the contract ending early, or the client suddenly deciding they hate my work, or an invoice getting held up in accounting and causing me to miss a mortgage payment. It's like being fired thirteen months ago just permanently broke some kind of switch in my brain, and now it's stuck in the 'on' position no matter what my actual employment status is. Even with the contract work coming in, the dread is just exactly the same as it was the day I lost my job. I just can't seem to turn it off.\n\nDoctor: When you start feeling anxious or overwhelmed by these thoughts, do you find yourself actively avoiding certain situations, tasks, or even people because you think they might trigger more of these worries?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. All the time now. It's actually becoming a huge problem, both professionally and personally, and I just... I don't know how to break out of it. \n\nLike with the contract work I was just telling you about... I'll have a deliverable due, right? Something standard, like a system architecture review or a migration plan. I know how to do it, I've done a million of them in my career. But I'll sit down to send the final file, and my brain just starts screaming at me. 'What if you missed a critical vulnerability? What if the client hates the formatting? What if they think your work is sloppy and they tell everyone in the Bethesda tech loop?' So... I just don't send it. I'll tell myself I just need to review it one more time tomorrow. And then tomorrow comes, and I do the exact same thing. I’ve literally delayed client deliverables for days because I just get completely stuck, paralyzed really, anticipating every single little thing that could possibly go wrong. \n\nAnd networking? Forget about it. In this freelance game, you have to network, but I've missed so many crucial follow-ups. I'll get someone's contact info, and then I'll just stare at a blank email draft for an hour, convinced that whatever I write is going to make me sound desperate or incompetent, until I just close the window and avoid them entirely. \n\nAnd the worst part is that it’s not just work. It’s bleeding into everything else. I used to play in this adult rec soccer league. Nothing crazy, just a bunch of guys trying not to pull a hamstring on Thursday nights, you know? We’d play, maybe grab a beer after. I've played with some of these guys for years. But I’ve completely pulled back from that. I haven't gone in over a month, and I've basically ghosted my friends there. \n\nAnd honestly... it's because I’m just so embarrassed. I'm embarrassed by who I am right now. Because I'm so wound up and exhausted all the time, I've gotten incredibly tense and irritable. I'll be out on the field and someone will miss a pass or make a stupid joke, and I'll just feel this massive flash of anger and snap at them. Or we'll be sitting around after the game, and I can't even participate in the conversation because I'm sitting there bouncing my leg, secretly doing mortgage math in my head, totally checked out. I just feel like I'm horrible to be around right now, so it's easier to just avoid them. To avoid everything. But then, of course, I sit at home alone and worry about the fact that I'm alienating all my friends, which just makes the whole thing worse. It's just this endless loop.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced sudden episodes where you feel intense fear or discomfort that peaks within minutes, along with physical symptoms like shortness of breath, chest pain, or dizziness?\n\nPatient: Sudden... no. Not sudden, and not really peaking like that. I mean, I know what you're asking about, like those moments where people feel like they're having a heart attack or can't catch their breath, right? I don't get that. It's actually kind of the opposite of sudden for me. It's not a spike, it's a plateau. A really, really high plateau that I just live on 24/7 now. \n\nI don't get dizzy, and I don't lose my breath. My chest... well, I guess my shoulders and chest get tight, but that's just because I'm literally clenching my muscles all day long from the stress. Like I was telling you with the soccer stuff, I'm just so physically tense all the time. But it's not a sudden pain or anything like that. It's not like I'm fine one minute and then suddenly gasping for air the next. \n\nIf I had to describe it in IT terms—because that's just how my brain works—it's not a sudden system crash where everything just goes black and all the alarms start blaring all at once. It's more like a memory leak. It just slowly, continuously consumes all my processing power in the background until the whole system is just dragging, barely functioning, running at max capacity, but it never actually reboots. It just grinds away, constantly. I wake up, and the dread is already just sitting there, and it stays with me while I make my coffee, while I check my emails... it never really peaks because it never really drops, if that makes sense. So, no, I don't have those sudden episodes with the dizziness or the breathing issues. It's just a constant, heavy dread that never really gives me a break to even know what a sudden peak would feel like.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself feeling down, losing interest in things you used to enjoy, or feeling like you're not good enough or that life isn't worth living?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I mean, I know I just said I stopped going to my soccer games, and I guess from the outside that probably looks exactly like losing interest, right? Like someone who’s just given up on their hobbies. But it’s really not that at all. I still want to play. I miss playing. I miss the guys. If I could just magically turn off this buzzing in my head, I’d be out on that field in a heartbeat. So it’s definitely not a lack of interest... it’s more like a surplus of panic. The interest is still there, it’s just completely buried under all the stress and the tension I was telling you about. \n\nAnd as for feeling down, or like life isn't worth living... no. Definitely not that. I don't have those kinds of dark thoughts about giving up or not wanting to be here. Honestly, it’s almost the exact opposite. If I didn't care, or if I thought life wasn't worth it, I wouldn't be spending twenty-four hours a day absolutely terrified of losing everything, you know? I’m fighting tooth and nail just to keep my head above water with these freelance contracts and the mortgage. You don't do that if you’ve given up. You’d just let the water take you. But I’m treading water so hard I’m exhausting myself. \n\nI mean, sure, I get frustrated. I get incredibly frustrated and demoralized that my brain is stuck in this loop, and I hate how tense I am all the time, but I don't feel worthless. I know I have value. I know I have skills—I built an entire IT department from the ground up at my last firm, I know what I'm capable of when my brain isn't sabotaging me. So, no, it's not a depression thing. I don't feel hopeless about life itself, I just feel completely hijacked by this constant, grinding worry about what's going to go wrong next.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to concentrate or stay focused because your mind is so consumed with worry?\n\nPatient: Concentrate? No, I don't... I don't think so. I mean, I guess it depends on what you mean by focus, but if we're talking about just the sheer ability to lock in on something? No, I haven't noticed any problems with that at all. I will stare at a network topology map, tracing every single data path, looking for vulnerabilities, for hours. Literally hours without blinking, without getting up to get a glass of water, without even noticing that the sun went down outside. \n\nSo the focus is definitely there. My brain isn't jumping around, you know? It's not like I'm easily distracted by a bird outside the window or a notification on my phone. It's just that the worry... it doesn't break my concentration, it kind of fuses with it. I get hyper-focused. Like, I'll be looking at a standard firewall configuration, and the thought will pop into my head: 'What if there's a zero-day exploit in this specific firmware version and the client gets ransomed six months from now and blames me?' And instead of losing my train of thought, I just drill down into that one specific, incredibly unlikely scenario with absolute laser precision. I'll spend three hours researching that one tiny 'what if' until my eyes are burning. \n\nSo I'm locked in. I'm completely, one-hundred-percent locked in. I can concentrate just fine. I just can't seem to concentrate on anything *but* the absolute worst-case scenarios. I'm focusing so hard on trying to prevent the disaster that I can't step back and just finish the basic task. Honestly, I kind of wish I *did* find it hard to concentrate sometimes. I know I have skills—I built an entire IT department from the ground up at my last firm, I know what I'm capable of when my brain isn't sabotaging me. So, no, it's not a depression thing. I don't feel hopeless about life itself, I just feel completely hijacked by this constant, grinding worry about what's going to go wrong next.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to concentrate or stay focused because your mind is so consumed with worry?\n\nPatient: Concentrate? No, I don't... I don't think so. I mean, I guess it depends on what you mean by focus, but if we're talking about just the sheer ability to lock in on something? No, I haven't noticed any problems with that at all. Actually, it’s kind of the exact opposite for me. \n\nWhen you're managing information systems, especially when you're doing the kind of high-level architecture reviews and migrations I do, your entire job is concentration. You have to be able to hold this massive, complex web of dependencies in your head all at once. And I can still do that. I can sit down at my desk—my home office, which I spent weeks setting up perfectly with the dual monitors and the standing desk, because if I'm going to be doing this freelance thing I need the environment to be totally optimized—but anyway, I'll sit there and I don't lose focus at all. I will stare at a network topology map, tracing every single data path, looking for vulnerabilities, for hours. Literally hours without blinking, without getting up to get a glass of water, without even noticing that the sun went down outside. \n\nSo the focus is definitely there. My brain isn't jumping around, you know? It's not like I'm easily distracted by a bird outside the window or a notification on my phone. It's just that the worry... it doesn't break my concentration, it kind of fuses with it. I get hyper-focused. Like, I'll be looking at a standard firewall configuration, and the thought will pop into my head: 'What if there's a zero-day exploit in this specific firmware version and the client gets ransomed six months from now and blames me?' And instead of losing my train of thought, I just drill down into that one specific, incredibly unlikely scenario with absolute laser precision. I'll spend three hours researching that one tiny 'what if' until my eyes are burning. \n\nSo I'm locked in. I'm completely, one-hundred-percent locked in. I can concentrate just fine. I just can't seem to concentrate on anything *but* the absolute worst-case scenarios. I'm focusing so hard on trying to prevent the disaster that I can't step back and just finish the basic task. Honestly, I kind of wish I *did* find it hard to concentrate sometimes. I wish I could just get distracted and lose my focus, just to get a five-minute break from my own brain, to be completely honest with you. But no, staying focused isn't the issue at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself excessively worrying about your physical health or specific bodily symptoms, even when there’s no clear medical cause for concern?\n\nPatient: My physical health? No... no, not really. I mean, I know some people get really hung up on every little ache and pain, going down those WebMD rabbit holes at two in the morning, but that’s just not me. \n\nLike I was saying earlier, I definitely have physical stuff going on—the tight shoulders, the chest tension—but I know exactly what that is. I don't sit around thinking I’m having a heart attack or that I have some rare neurological disease. I know it's just the stress. It’s just my muscles being locked up from sitting at my desk for fourteen hours a day, clenching my jaw while I try to keep all these freelance plates spinning. If my stomach hurts, I just figure I drank too much coffee. If my head aches, I know it's because I've been staring at dual monitors since 6:00 AM. \n\nHonestly, I had my annual physical back in... I want to say October? Yeah, October, right around the time I was doing this massive server migration for a client that ended up being a total nightmare. But my regular doctor ran all the standard blood panels, checked my cholesterol, my blood pressure—which was a little elevated, but nothing crazy—and everything was fine. And I just accepted that. I didn't second-guess him or demand an MRI or anything. \n\nIf I'm being completely honest, I feel like I don't even have the bandwidth to worry about my health. It’s like my brain has allocated one hundred percent of its resources to worrying about my career, the mortgage, the contracts, and the fear of making some catastrophic error in a client's infrastructure. There is literally no RAM left over to worry about a weird mole or a cough. My wife was actually just saying the other day that I need to get my eyes checked because I've been squinting at my screens, and I just told her, \"Look, if my vision goes, it goes, but right now I have to make sure this AWS deployment doesn't fail.\" Obviously, I don't want to lose my vision, but you get what I mean. My physical health just doesn't even register on my dashboard of imminent catastrophes. All the alarms in my head are strictly tied to my work and my finances, so no, I don't really obsess over my body or get anxious about medical stuff at all. It's just not where my worry lives.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble falling asleep or staying asleep at night because your mind is racing with worries?\n\nPatient: Oh, sleep. Yeah. That’s... that’s a whole other disaster area entirely. \n\nMost nights, I just lie there in the dark. My wife will be dead asleep next to me, breathing perfectly normally, and I’m just staring at the ceiling fan, completely wired. My brain just decides that midnight is the perfect time to do a full, comprehensive review of everything I might have messed up recently. I'll lie there replaying interview answers from client pitches I did three days ago. I'll literally reconstruct the entire conversation in my head, analyzing every pause, every inflection. Like, did I sound too desperate when we talked about my hourly rate? Did I explain that cloud migration strategy clearly enough, or did the hiring manager think I was out of my depth? I just play the tape over and over and over again until I'm physically tense under the blankets.\n\nAnd then, if my body finally just gives out and I do manage to drift off, it’s not like it's a deep, restful sleep. I’ll jolt awake, my heart will be racing, and I’ll roll over to check the clock, and it is always, without fail, 2:00 or 3:00 a.m. Every single time. And once I see that glowing number, the countdown math starts. I'll think, 'Okay, if I can just force myself back to sleep right this second, I can still get three and a half hours before the alarm.' But of course, doing that mental math just wakes me up even more, and then the worry loop about the mortgage and the freelance contracts boots right back up. \n\nBut honestly, the most frustrating part is that even on the rare occasions where I get what should technically be enough sleep—like if I look at my phone in the morning and I was actually in bed, unconscious, for seven or eight hours—it doesn't even matter. I don't feel rested at all. I wake up feeling exactly like I spent the entire night on call for a massive system outage. \n\nI don't know if you've ever had to deal with that in your line of work, but in IT, when a critical server goes down at 3 a.m. and you're just running on pure adrenaline and panic, trying to patch the infrastructure before the whole company logs on at 8 a.m... you feel completely hollowed out and electrified at the same time. That’s how I feel every single morning when I open my eyes. My brain is already red-lining, I'm exhausted, and I haven't even thrown the covers off yet. So, yeah... sleep isn't really sleep for me right now. It's just a different room for me to worry in.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Six months? God, I wish it was only six months. No, if we're talking about a timeline, you have to go back quite a bit further than that. Try thirteen months. It’s been going on for about thirteen months now. That was... well, that was when I was fired from my last full-time position. 

I still remember the exact feeling in the room. I had been there for years, built their entire backend infrastructure from the ground up, and then management just brings in some outside efficiency consultants, and suddenly my salary is a redundancy. Just like that. Hand over your laptop, give us your badge, here’s a cardboard box. And I can tell you, the exact second I walked out of that building and the glass doors slid shut behind me, that’s when this... this engine of worry in my head just fired up. Instead of obsessing over being unemployed, I started obsessing over every single freelance contract. What if the client doesn't renew? So, no, there haven't been any breaks or periods where I felt at ease. It’s been a solid thirteen months of just relentless, grinding pressure, every single day, whether I have a contract in hand or not. I honestly don't even remember what being 'at ease' is supposed to feel like at this point. It's just been one continuous, thirteen-month-long loop.

The worry just finds me wherever I am, whatever I'm doing, and it just bounces from my finances to my career to my reputation without giving me a single second to breathe.\n\nDoctor: When these anxious thoughts come up, do you also notice any physical symptoms, like restlessness, trouble sleeping, muscle tension, or a racing heart?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. It's not just in my head, it's... it's like my whole body is involved in this, you know? The tension is just constant. I feel completely wound up almost all day long. My shoulders, for instance—they are just locked tight up around my ears from the minute I wake up. It’s like I’m physically bracing myself, just constantly ready for some terrible news to drop, even when there's absolutely no reason to expect any. \n\nAnd the restlessness, it’s just... it's exhausting. I can't just sit still anymore. If I'm on a conference call—even a totally routine one where I’m just listening in and nothing is actually happening—I’ll be sitting there just bouncing my leg up and down like a jackhammer under the desk. Or if I'm off camera, I'll literally just pace back and forth across my home office. I'll walk from the desk to the window, back to the desk, into the hallway, just pacing and pacing while I'm trying to listen to server updates or whatever. \n\nIt’s like there’s this electric current just running through me all the time and I have to move to bleed some of it off, but it never really goes away. I just feel so physically keyed up, like a spring wound way too tight, and no matter how much I pace around the house or try to stretch out my neck and shoulders, that locked-in, braced feeling just stays right there. It's like my body is reacting to an emergency that isn't even happening, and I just can't get it to stand down.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you worry excessively about a variety of things in your life, even when there's no clear reason to be concerned, and does it feel like this worry is hard to control or stop, even when you try?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... yes, absolutely. It's exactly like that. It's just this constant, unending barrage of things to worry about, and I really, truly cannot control it. I try, I really do. I'll tell myself, 'Okay, just focus on this one task,' but it never works. It's like my brain has a mind of its own, if that makes sense. \n\nLike I was saying before, it's never just one thing. My mind keeps jumping from my current contract work, which is stressful enough on its own, right? But then it doesn't stop there. It immediately leaps over to my mortgage payments, and I start doing all this math in my head about what happens if a contract falls through and I can't make the payment, and then I'm picturing foreclosure. And then, as if that's not enough, it pivots entirely to my reputation. I'll just sit there obsessing over whether people in the Bethesda tech circles—which is a really tight-knit community, everybody talks, you know?—I'll worry that they think I’m damaged goods now because of the messy divorce, or that they think I'm losing my edge because I'm distracted. \n\nIt just bounces from one disaster to the next. And there doesn't even need to be a real reason for it! Like that recruiter email I mentioned. It was just delayed. Probably the guy was just at lunch or stuck on a call. But to me, in that moment? That delayed email felt like absolute, undeniable proof that my whole career is closing down. I sat there and basically planned out my entire professional funeral just because an email was an hour late. And I know how irrational that sounds when I say it out loud to you, I really do. I'm a logical guy, I work in IT, I deal with facts and systems. But when the worry hits, all that logic just goes right out the window, and I can't stop the spiral no matter how hard I try to reason with myself.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this constant and uncontrollable worry, and was there a specific event or change in your life that seemed to trigger it?\n\nPatient: Well, if I really trace it back... it’s been a little over a year now. Thirteen months, to be exact. Thirteen months of just... this. That was when I got let go from my old firm. I was fired, and honestly, that was the absolute catalyst for all of this. I had been there for a long time, you know? I had built up my department, I knew the systems inside and out, and then suddenly—just, bam, I'm out. The floor completely dropped out from under me. \n\nAt first, I thought the constant worrying was just a normal reaction to losing my job. I mean, anyone would be stressed about that, right? You lose your primary income, you lose your daily routine, you immediately start doing the math on how many months you can survive before the savings run dry. I remember sitting at my kitchen table the morning after it happened, just staring at my laptop, trying to figure out how to even begin updating my resume after all those years, and that's when this... this engine of worry just turned on in my head. \n\nBut the craziest part, and the part that really exhausts me, is that I thought it would stop once I got back on my feet. I really, truly believed that. I told myself, 'Okay, I just need to find a new gig, get some income flowing again, and my brain will finally settle down.' But I did find work. I transitioned over to doing contract work, managing some system migrations on a freelance basis, and it keeps the lights on. But the worry? It never let up. Not even a fraction of a bit. It didn't go away when I signed that first contract. \n\nIf anything, it just shifted its focus. Instead of worrying about not having a job at all, now I'm obsessing over the contract ending early, or the client suddenly deciding they hate my work, or an invoice getting held up in accounting and causing me to miss a mortgage payment. It's like being fired thirteen months ago just permanently broke some kind of switch in my brain, and now it's stuck in the 'on' position no matter what my actual employment status is. Even with the contract work coming in, the dread is just exactly the same as it was the day I lost my job. I just can't seem to turn it off.\n\nDoctor: When you start feeling anxious or overwhelmed by these thoughts, do you find yourself actively avoiding certain situations, tasks, or even people because you think they might trigger more of these worries?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. All the time now. It's actually becoming a huge problem, both professionally and personally, and I just... I don't know how to break out of it. \n\nLike with the contract work I was just telling you about... I'll have a deliverable due, right? Something standard, like a system architecture review or a migration plan. I know how to do it, I've done a million of them in my career. But I'll sit down to send the final file, and my brain just starts screaming at me. 'What if you missed a critical vulnerability? What if the client hates the formatting? What if they think your work is sloppy and they tell everyone in the Bethesda tech loop?' So... I just don't send it. I'll tell myself I just need to review it one more time tomorrow. And then tomorrow comes, and I do the exact same thing. I’ve literally delayed client deliverables for days because I just get completely stuck, paralyzed really, anticipating every single little thing that could possibly go wrong. \n\nAnd networking? Forget about it. In this freelance game, you have to network, but I've missed so many crucial follow-ups. I'll get someone's contact info, and then I'll just stare at a blank email draft for an hour, convinced that whatever I write is going to make me sound desperate or incompetent, until I just close the window and avoid them entirely. \n\nAnd the worst part is that it’s not just work. It’s bleeding into everything else. I used to play in this adult rec soccer league. Nothing crazy, just a bunch of guys trying not to pull a hamstring on Thursday nights, you know? We’d play, maybe grab a beer after. I've played with some of these guys for years. But I’ve completely pulled back from that. I haven't gone in over a month, and I've basically ghosted my friends there. \n\nAnd honestly... it's because I’m just so embarrassed. I'm embarrassed by who I am right now. Because I'm so wound up and exhausted all the time, I've gotten incredibly tense and irritable. I'll be out on the field and someone will miss a pass or make a stupid joke, and I'll just feel this massive flash of anger and snap at them. Or we'll be sitting around after the game, and I can't even participate in the conversation because I'm sitting there bouncing my leg, secretly doing mortgage math in my head, totally checked out. I just feel like I'm horrible to be around right now, so it's easier to just avoid them. To avoid everything. But then, of course, I sit at home alone and worry about the fact that I'm alienating all my friends, which just makes the whole thing worse. It's just this endless loop.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced sudden episodes where you feel intense fear or discomfort that peaks within minutes, along with physical symptoms like shortness of breath, chest pain, or dizziness?\n\nPatient: Sudden... no. Not sudden, and not really peaking like that. I mean, I know what you're asking about, like those moments where people feel like they're having a heart attack or can't catch their breath, right? I don't get that. It's actually kind of the opposite of sudden for me. It's not a spike, it's a plateau. A really, really high plateau that I just live on 24/7 now. \n\nI don't get dizzy, and I don't lose my breath. My chest... well, I guess my shoulders and chest get tight, but that's just because I'm literally clenching my muscles all day long from the stress. Like I was telling you with the soccer stuff, I'm just so physically tense all the time. But it's not a sudden pain or anything like that. It's not like I'm fine one minute and then suddenly gasping for air the next. \n\nIf I had to describe it in IT terms—because that's just how my brain works—it's not a sudden system crash where everything just goes black and all the alarms start blaring all at once. It's more like a memory leak. It just slowly, continuously consumes all my processing power in the background until the whole system is just dragging, barely functioning, running at max capacity, but it never actually reboots. It just grinds away, constantly. I wake up, and the dread is already just sitting there, and it stays with me while I make my coffee, while I check my emails... it never really peaks because it never really drops, if that makes sense. So, no, I don't have those sudden episodes with the dizziness or the breathing issues. It's just a constant, heavy dread that never really gives me a break to even know what a sudden peak would feel like.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself feeling down, losing interest in things you used to enjoy, or feeling like you're not good enough or that life isn't worth living?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I mean, I know I just said I stopped going to my soccer games, and I guess from the outside that probably looks exactly like losing interest, right? Like someone who’s just given up on their hobbies. But it’s really not that at all. I still want to play. I miss playing. I miss the guys. If I could just magically turn off this buzzing in my head, I’d be out on that field in a heartbeat. So it’s definitely not a lack of interest... it’s more like a surplus of panic. The interest is still there, it’s just completely buried under all the stress and the tension I was telling you about. \n\nAnd as for feeling down, or like life isn't worth living... no. Definitely not that. I don't have those kinds of dark thoughts about giving up or not wanting to be here. Honestly, it’s almost the exact opposite. If I didn't care, or if I thought life wasn't worth it, I wouldn't be spending twenty-four hours a day absolutely terrified of losing everything, you know? I’m fighting tooth and nail just to keep my head above water with these freelance contracts and the mortgage. You don't do that if you’ve given up. You’d just let the water take you. But I’m treading water so hard I’m exhausting myself. \n\nI mean, sure, I get frustrated. I get incredibly frustrated and demoralized that my brain is stuck in this loop, and I hate how tense I am all the time, but I don't feel worthless. I know I have value. I know I have skills—I built an entire IT department from the ground up at my last firm, I know what I'm capable of when my brain isn't sabotaging me. So, no, it's not a depression thing. I don't feel hopeless about life itself, I just feel completely hijacked by this constant, grinding worry about what's going to go wrong next.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to concentrate or stay focused because your mind is so consumed with worry?\n\nPatient: Concentrate? No, I don't... I don't think so. I mean, I guess it depends on what you mean by focus, but if we're talking about just the sheer ability to lock in on something? No, I haven't noticed any problems with that at all. Actually, it’s kind of the exact opposite for me. \n\nWhen you're managing information systems, especially when you're doing the kind of high-level architecture reviews and migrations I do, your entire job is concentration. You have to be able to hold this massive, complex web of dependencies in your head all at once. And I can still do that. I can sit down at my desk—my home office, which I spent weeks setting up perfectly with the dual monitors and the standing desk, because if I'm going to be doing this freelance thing I need the environment to be totally optimized—but anyway, I'll sit there and I don't lose focus at all. I will stare at a network topology map, tracing every single data path, looking for vulnerabilities, for hours. Literally hours without blinking, without getting up to get a glass of water, without even noticing that the sun went down outside. \n\nSo the focus is definitely there. My brain isn't jumping around, you know? It's not like I'm easily distracted by a bird outside the window or a notification on my phone. It's just that the worry... it doesn't break my concentration, it kind of fuses with it. I get hyper-focused. Like, I'll be looking at a standard firewall configuration, and the thought will pop into my head: 'What if there's a zero-day exploit in this specific firmware version and the client gets ransomed six months from now and blames me?' And instead of losing my train of thought, I just drill down into that one specific, incredibly unlikely scenario with absolute laser precision. I'll spend three hours researching that one tiny 'what if' until my eyes are burning. \n\nSo I'm locked in. I'm completely, one-hundred-percent locked in. I can concentrate just fine. I just can't seem to concentrate on anything *but* the absolute worst-case scenarios. I'm focusing so hard on trying to prevent the disaster that I can't step back and just finish the basic task. Honestly, I kind of wish I *did* find it hard to concentrate sometimes. I wish I could just get distracted and lose my focus, just to get a five-minute break from my own brain, to be completely honest with you. It’s just my muscles being locked up from sitting at my desk for fourteen hours a day, clenching my jaw while I try to keep all these freelance plates spinning. If my stomach hurts, I just figure I drank too much coffee. If my head aches, I know it's because I've been staring at dual monitors since 6:00 AM. \n\nHonestly, I had my annual physical back in... I want to say October? Yeah, October, right around the time I was doing this massive server migration for a client that ended up being a total nightmare. But my regular doctor ran all the standard blood panels, checked my cholesterol, my blood pressure—which was a little elevated, but nothing crazy—and everything was fine. And I just accepted that. I didn't second-guess him or demand an MRI or anything. \n\nIf I'm being completely honest, I feel like I don't even have the bandwidth to worry about my health. It’s like my brain has allocated one hundred percent of its resources to worrying about my career, the mortgage, the contracts, and the fear of making some catastrophic error in a client's infrastructure. There is literally no RAM left over to worry about a weird mole or a cough. My physical health just doesn't even register on my dashboard of imminent catastrophes. All the alarms in my head are strictly tied to my work and my finances, so no, I don't really obsess over my body or get anxious about medical stuff at all. It's just not where my worry lives.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble falling asleep or staying asleep at night because your mind is racing with worries?\n\nPatient: Oh, sleep. Yeah. That’s... that’s a whole other disaster area entirely. \n\nMost nights, I just lie there in the dark. My wife will be dead asleep next to me, breathing perfectly normally, and I’m just staring at the ceiling fan, completely wired. My brain just decides that midnight is the perfect time to do a full, comprehensive review of everything I might have messed up recently. I'll lie there replaying interview answers from client pitches I did three days ago. I'll literally reconstruct the entire conversation in my head, analyzing every pause, every inflection. Like, did I sound too desperate when we talked about my hourly rate? Did I explain that cloud migration strategy clearly enough, or did the hiring manager think I was out of my depth? I just play the tape over and over and over again until I'm physically tense under the blankets.\n\nAnd then, if my body finally just gives out and I do manage to drift off, it’s not like it's a deep, restful sleep. I’ll jolt awake, my heart will be racing, and I’ll roll over to check the clock, and it is always, without fail, 2:00 or 3:00 a.m. Every single time. And once I see that glowing number, the countdown math starts. I'll think, 'Okay, if I can just force myself back to sleep right this second, I can still get three and a half hours before the alarm.' But of course, doing that mental math just wakes me up even more, and then the worry loop about the mortgage and the freelance contracts boots right back up. \n\nBut honestly, the most frustrating part is that even on the rare occasions where I get what should technically be enough sleep—like if I look at my phone in the morning and I was actually in bed, unconscious, for seven or eight hours—it doesn't even matter. I don't feel rested at all. I wake up feeling exactly like I spent the entire night on call for a massive system outage. \n\nI don't know if you've ever had to deal with that in your line of work, but in IT, when a critical server goes down at 3 a.m. and you're just running on pure adrenaline and panic, trying to patch the infrastructure before the whole company logs on at 8 a.m... you feel completely hollowed out and electrified at the same time. That’s how I feel every single morning when I open my eyes. My brain is already red-lining, I'm exhausted, and I haven't even thrown the covers off yet. So, yeah... sleep isn't really sleep for me right now. It's just a different room for me to worry in.\n\nDoctor: Have these problems with constant worry and physical tension been present for most days over the past six months, or have there been significant breaks in between when you felt more at ease?\n\nPatient: Six months? God, I wish it was only six months. No, if we're talking about a timeline, you have to go back quite a bit further than that. Try thirteen months. It’s been going on for about thirteen months now. That was... well, that was when I was fired from my last full-time position. \n\nI still remember the exact feeling in the room. I had been there for years, built their entire backend infrastructure from the ground up, and then management just brings in some outside efficiency consultants, and suddenly my salary is a redundancy. Just like that. Hand over your laptop, give us your badge, here’s a cardboard box. And I can tell you, the exact second I walked out of that building and the glass doors slid shut behind me, that’s when this... this engine of worry in my head just fired up. And the physical tension, the knot in my chest, the tight shoulders... it all just locked into place right then and there. \n\nAnd you asked if there were breaks? If I felt at ease at all? No. Absolutely none. Not for a single day. I mean, logically, you would think that once I got back on my feet, things would calm down, right? Like, okay, the immediate crisis is over, you can breathe now. But it never really let up, even after I found contract work. I started picking up these freelance architecture jobs, and yeah, it keeps the lights on, it pays the mortgage, but the tension didn't go anywhere. It just... it just mutated. Instead of obsessing over being unemployed, I started obsessing over every single freelance contract. What if the client doesn't renew? What if I miss a deliverable because I'm juggling too many projects? What if the freelance market dries up next quarter and I'm right back to zero? \n\nSo, no, there haven't been any breaks or periods where I felt at ease. It’s been a solid thirteen months of just relentless, grinding pressure, every single day, whether I have a contract in hand or not. I honestly don't even remember what being 'at ease' is supposed to feel like at this point. It's just been one continuous, thirteen-month-long loop.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Well, it’s... it's really everything. I mean, yes, losing the job was the spark, but the fire just spread everywhere. It doesn't stay neatly contained to just the big career changes. It’s like this relentless pinball machine in my head. My mind keeps jumping from the immediate contract work I’m doing, to panicking about the mortgage payments... because, you know, we refinanced a while back thinking my old salary was a permanent fixture, and now every time the end of the month approaches, I start running these catastrophic mental spreadsheets about how many months we have left before we lose the house. 

But then it doesn't even stop at the finances. It jumps to what people are thinking about me. The Bethesda tech circles, for instance, they are surprisingly small and insular. Everybody knows everybody's business. So I'll just sit there agonizing over whether people in the Bethesda tech circles think I’m damaged goods now. I'll convince myself that I'm the subject of some whispered conversation at a networking event I didn't even go to, that everyone has secretly decided I'm a liability. 

And it attaches to the smallest, most everyday things, too, which is just exhausting. Like, if I'm waiting on a response from someone. If there's just a delayed email from a recruiter—even if it's just a few hours late—I don't just assume they stepped away from their desk or they're busy in a meeting. A delayed email can feel like absolute, undeniable proof that my whole career is closing down. My brain just decides that the silence means they found out I'm a failure, they've blacklisted me, and the door is permanently shut. Over an email delay of maybe three hours! So, yeah, it’s not just the big picture stuff. It’s the everyday things, the minor details, the bills, the emails... it all just gets fed right into the same massive worry engine.

I can sit down at my desk—my home office, which I spent weeks setting up perfectly with the dual monitors and the standing desk, because if I'm going to be doing this freelance thing I need the environment to be totally optimized—but anyway, I'll sit there and I don't lose focus at all. It's just that the worry... it doesn't break my concentration, it kind of fuses with it. I get hyper-focused. Like, I'll be looking at a standard firewall configuration, and the thought will pop into my head: 'What if there's a zero-day exploit in this specific firmware version and the client gets ransomed six months from now and blames me?' I'll spend three hours researching that one tiny 'what if' until my eyes are burning. \n\nSo I'm locked in. I'm completely, one-hundred-percent locked in. I can concentrate just fine. Honestly, I kind of wish I *did* find it hard to concentrate sometimes. Honestly, I kind of wish I *did* find it hard to concentrate sometimes. I wish I could just get distracted and lose my focus, just to get a five-minute break from my own brain, to be completely honest with you. But no, staying focused isn't the issue at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself excessively worrying about your physical health or specific bodily symptoms, even when there’s no clear medical cause for concern?\n\nPatient: My physical health? No... no, not really. I mean, I know some people get really hung up on every little ache and pain, going down those WebMD rabbit holes at two in the morning, but that’s just not me. \n\nLike I was saying earlier, I definitely have physical stuff going on—the tight shoulders, the chest tension—but I know exactly what that is. I don't sit around thinking I’m having a heart attack or that I have some rare neurological disease. I know it's just the stress. It’s just my muscles being locked up from sitting at my desk for fourteen hours a day, clenching my jaw while I try to keep all these freelance plates spinning. If my stomach hurts, I just figure I drank too much coffee. If my head aches, I know it's because I've been staring at dual monitors since 6:00 AM. \n\nHonestly, I had my annual physical back in... I want to say October? Yeah, October, right around the time I was doing this massive server migration for a client that ended up being a total nightmare. But my regular doctor ran all the standard blood panels, checked my cholesterol, my blood pressure—which was a little elevated, but nothing crazy—and everything was fine. And I just accepted that. I didn't second-guess him or demand an MRI or anything. \n\nIf I'm being completely honest, I feel like I don't even have the bandwidth to worry about my health. It’s like my brain has allocated one hundred percent of its resources to worrying about my career, the mortgage, the contracts, and the fear of making some catastrophic error in a client's infrastructure. There is literally no RAM left over to worry about a weird mole or a cough. My wife was actually just saying the other day that I need to get my eyes checked because I've been squinting at my screens, and I just told her, \"Look, if my vision goes, it goes, but right now I have to make sure this AWS deployment doesn't fail.\" Obviously, I don't want to lose my vision, but you get what I mean. My physical health just doesn't even register on my dashboard of imminent catastrophes. All the alarms in my head are strictly tied to my work and my finances, so no, I don't really obsess over my body or get anxious about medical stuff at all. It's just not where my worry lives.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble falling asleep or staying asleep at night because your mind is racing with worries?\n\nPatient: Oh, sleep. Yeah. That’s... that’s a whole other disaster area entirely. \n\nMost nights, I just lie there in the dark. My wife will be dead asleep next to me, breathing perfectly normally, and I’m just staring at the ceiling fan, completely wired. My brain just decides that midnight is the perfect time to do a full, comprehensive review of everything I might have messed up recently. I'll lie there replaying interview answers from client pitches I did three days ago. I'll literally reconstruct the entire conversation in my head, analyzing every pause, every inflection. Like, did I sound too desperate when we talked about my hourly rate? Did I explain that cloud migration strategy clearly enough, or did the hiring manager think I was out of my depth? I just play the tape over and over and over again until I'm physically tense under the blankets.\n\nAnd then, if my body finally just gives out and I do manage to drift off, it’s not like it's a deep, restful sleep. I’ll jolt awake, my heart will be racing, and I’ll roll over to check the clock, and it is always, without fail, 2:00 or 3:00 a.m. Every single time. And once I see that glowing number, the countdown math starts. I'll think, 'Okay, if I can just force myself back to sleep right this second, I can still get three and a half hours before the alarm.' But of course, doing that mental math just wakes me up even more, and then the worry loop about the mortgage and the freelance contracts boots right back up. \n\nBut honestly, the most frustrating part is that even on the rare occasions where I get what should technically be enough sleep—like if I look at my phone in the morning and I was actually in bed, unconscious, for seven or eight hours—it doesn't even matter. I don't feel rested at all. I wake up feeling exactly like I spent the entire night on call for a massive system outage. \n\nI don't know if you've ever had to deal with that in your line of work, but in IT, when a critical server goes down at 3 a.m. and you're just running on pure adrenaline and panic, trying to patch the infrastructure before the whole company logs on at 8 a.m... you feel completely hollowed out and electrified at the same time. That’s how I feel every single morning when I open my eyes. My brain is already red-lining, I'm exhausted, and I haven't even thrown the covers off yet. So, yeah... sleep isn't really sleep for me right now. It's just a different room for me to worry in.\n\nDoctor: Have these problems with constant worry and physical tension been present for most days over the past six months, or have there been significant breaks in between when you felt more at ease?\n\nPatient: Six months? God, I wish it was only six months. No, if we're talking about a timeline, you have to go back quite a bit further than that. Try thirteen months. It’s been going on for about thirteen months now. That was... well, that was when I was fired from my last full-time position. \n\nI still remember the exact feeling in the room. I had been there for years, built their entire backend infrastructure from the ground up, and then management just brings in some outside efficiency consultants, and suddenly my salary is a redundancy. Just like that. Hand over your laptop, give us your badge, here’s a cardboard box. And I can tell you, the exact second I walked out of that building and the glass doors slid shut behind me, that’s when this... this engine of worry in my head just fired up. And the physical tension, the knot in my chest, the tight shoulders... it all just locked into place right then and there. \n\nAnd you asked if there were breaks? If I felt at ease at all? No. Absolutely none. Not for a single day. I mean, logically, you would think that once I got back on my feet, things would calm down, right? Like, okay, the immediate crisis is over, you can breathe now. But it never really let up, even after I found contract work. I started picking up these freelance architecture jobs, and yeah, it keeps the lights on, it pays the mortgage, but the tension didn't go anywhere. It just... it just mutated. Instead of obsessing over being unemployed, I started obsessing over every single freelance contract. What if the client doesn't renew? What if I miss a deliverable because I'm juggling too many projects? What if the freelance market dries up next quarter and I'm right back to zero? \n\nSo, no, there haven't been any breaks or periods where I felt at ease. It’s been a solid thirteen months of just relentless, grinding pressure, every single day, whether I have a contract in hand or not. I honestly don't even remember what being 'at ease' is supposed to feel like at this point. It's just been one continuous, thirteen-month-long loop.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your anxiety tends to focus on a wide range of everyday situations, even small ones, rather than being limited mainly to specific events related to losing your job or the changes in your career?\n\nPatient: Well, it’s... it's really everything. I mean, yes, losing the job was the spark, but the fire just spread everywhere. It doesn't stay neatly contained to just the big career changes. It’s like this relentless pinball machine in my head. My mind keeps jumping from the immediate contract work I’m doing, to panicking about the mortgage payments... because, you know, we refinanced a while back thinking my old salary was a permanent fixture, and now every time the end of the month approaches, I start running these catastrophic mental spreadsheets about how many months we have left before we lose the house. \n\nBut then it doesn't even stop at the finances. It jumps to what people are thinking about me. The Bethesda tech circles, for instance, they are surprisingly small and insular. Everybody knows everybody's business. So I'll just sit there agonizing over whether people in the Bethesda tech circles think I’m damaged goods now. I'll convince myself that I'm the subject of some whispered conversation at a networking event I didn't even go to, that everyone has secretly decided I'm a liability. \n\nAnd it attaches to the smallest, most everyday things, too, which is just exhausting. Like, if I'm waiting on a response from someone. If there's just a delayed email from a recruiter—even if it's just a few hours late—I don't just assume they stepped away from their desk or they're busy in a meeting. A delayed email can feel like absolute, undeniable proof that my whole career is closing down. My brain just decides that the silence means they found out I'm a failure, they've blacklisted me, and the door is permanently shut. Over an email delay of maybe three hours! So, yeah, it’s not just the big picture stuff. It’s the everyday things, the minor details, the bills, the emails... it all just gets fed right into the same massive worry engine."
  }
